Master Candidate:

Pietro Gallato

Supervisor:

Prof. Nicola Garofalo

Academic Year

2021-2022

05/10/2022

“If people do not believe that mathematics is simple, it is only because they do not realize how complicated life is.”

— John Von Neumann

Abstract

This thesis studies the extension problem for higher-order fractional powers of the heat operator
H=\Delta-\partial_{t}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Specifically, given
s>0
and indicating with
[s]
its integral part, we study the following degenerate partial differential equation in the thick space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}_{y}^{+}
,

\mathscr{H}^{[s]+1}U=\left(\partial_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}+H\right)^{[s]+1}U=0.

(1)

The connection between the Bessel parameter
a
in (
1
) and the fractional parameter
s>0
is given by the equation

a=1-2(s-[s]).

When
s\in(0,1)
this equation reduces to the well-known relation
a=1-2s
, and in such case (
1
) becomes the famous Caffarelli-Silvestre extension problem. Generalising their result, in this thesis we show that the nonlocal operator
(-H)^{\,s}
can be realised as the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map associated with the solution
U
of the extension equation (
1
).

In this thesis we systematically exploit the evolutive semigroup
\{P_{\tau}^{H}\}_{\tau>0}
, associated with the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\partial_{\tau}u-Hu=0\\
u((x,t),0)=f(x,t).\end{cases}

This approach provides a powerful tool in analysis, and it has the twofold advantage of allowing an independent treatment of several complex calculations involving the Fourier transform, while at same time extending to frameworks where the Fourier transform is not available.

Chapter 1
Introduction

In his visionary papers
18
and
19
Marcel Riesz introduced the fractional powers of the Laplacean in Euclidean and Lorentzian space, developed the calculus of these nonlocal operators and studied the Dirichlet and Cauchy problems for respectively
(-\Delta)^{s}
and
(\partial_{tt}-\Delta)^{s}
.

Pseudo-differential operators such as
(-\Delta)^{s}
,
(\partial_{tt}-\Delta)^{s}
,
(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{s}
play an important role in many branches of the applied sciences ranging from fluid dynamics, to elasticity and to quantum mechanics.

Our objective in this thesis is studying the extension problem for higher-order fractional powers of the heat operator
H=\Delta-\partial_{t}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Specifically, given
s>0
and indicating with
[s]
its integral part, we study the following degenerate partial differential equation in the thick space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}_{y}^{+}
,

\mathscr{H}^{[s]+1}U=\left(\partial_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}+H\right)^{[s]+1}U=0.

(1.1)

The connection between the Bessel parameter
a
in (
1.1
) and the fractional non-integer parameter
s>0
is given by the equation

a=1-2(s-[s]).

When
s\in(0,1)
this equation reduces to the well-known relation
a=1-2s
, and in such case (
1.1
) becomes the famous Caffarelli-Silvestre extension problem. Generalising their result, in this thesis we show that the nonlocal operator
(-H)^{s}
can be realised as the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map associated with the solution
U
of the extension equation (
1.1
).

A list of the topics covered by this thesis is provided by the table of contents. Diving deeper into the details:

1.

In Chapter
2
we are presenting some of the most basic aspects of the operator
(-\Delta)^{s}
, a complete introduction of which is available at
11
. In particular we have

•

In Section
2.1
we introduce the main pointwise definition of the nonlocal operator
(-\Delta)^{s}
, see (
2.7
) below. In Proposition
2.1.8
we show that the definition (
2.7
) implies a decay at infinity of the fractional Laplacean that plays an important role in its analysis.

•

Section
2.2
constitutes a brief interlude on two important protagonist of classical analysis which also play a central role in this chapter: the Fourier transform and Bessel functions. These two classical subjects are inextricably connected. One the one hand, the Bessel functions are eigenfunctions of the Laplacean. On the other, they also appear as the Fourier transform of the measure carried by the unit sphere. In this connection, and since it is a recurrent ingredient in this note, we recall the classical Fourier-Bessel integral formula due to Bochner, see Theorem
2.2.4
below.

•

Section
2.3
opens with the proof of Proposition
2.3.1
, which describes the action of
(-\Delta)^{s}
on the Fourier transform side. This result proves an important fact: the fractional Laplacean is a
pseudo-differential operator
. A basic consequence of Proposition
2.3.1
is the semigroup property in Corollary
2.3.2
and the ”integration by parts” Lemma
2.3.3
, which shows that
(-\Delta)^{s}
is a symmetric operator. We close the section with the computation in Proposition
2.3.4
of the normalization constant
\gamma(n,s)
in the pointwise definition (
2.7
).

•

In Section
2.4
we want to find the fundamental solution of
(-\Delta)^{s}
, i.e., proving Theorem
2.4.4
. This can be done in several ways, but we choose to exploit the tools provided in
11
.

•

Section
2.5
presents in detail the central theme of the analysis of the fractional Laplacean: the extension problem of Caffarelli and Silvestre (
2.85
). We construct the Poisson kernel for the extension operator, and provide two proofs of (
2.86
), which characterizes
(-\Delta)^{s}
as the weighted Dirichlet-to-Neumann map of the extension problem. The extension procedure is a very powerful tool which has been applied so far in many different directions, and it is hardly possible to accurately describe the impact of this paper in the field.

2.

In Chapter
3
we study the fractional operators, in particular
(-\Delta)^{s}
and
(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{s}
, with the systematic use of the heat semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. The semigroup approach provides a powerful tool in analysis and has a twofold advantage. On one hand it allows a treatment independent of several complex calculations involving the Fourier transform and, more importantly, it extends to frameworks in which the Fourier transform is not available. References on the methods and tools exploited in this chapter can be found at
10
. In particular we have

•

In Section
3.1
we define in (
3.2
) the heat semigroup and its main properties. The name is justified by the fact that the function
u(x,t)=P_{t}f(x)
solves the Cauchy problem for the heat equation
\partial_{t}u-\Delta u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

•

Section
3.2
opens with the Proposition
3.2.1
, which basically shows the ultracontractivity property of the heat semigroup.

•

In Section
3.3
we define the fractional Laplacean according to the formulation of Balakrishnan in Definition
3.3.1
. We then proceed with the proof of some properties of the fractional Laplacean, similarly to what is done in Chapter
2
, but now leveraging the advantages that the heat semigroup grants.

•

In Section
3.4
we show that Balakrishnan’s definition of the nonlocal operator
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
coincides with that introduced by M. Riesz in
18
. Subsequently, we analyse the asymptotic behaviour of this operator as
\alpha\nearrow 2
and we show that, unsurprisingly, in the limit we obtain the negative of the Laplace operator
\Delta
.

•

In semigroup theory a procedure for forming a new semigroup from a given one is that of the evolution semigroup. In Section
3.5
we exploit this idea to introduce a new semigroup that will be used as a building block for: (i) defining the fractional powers of the heat operator
H=\Delta-\partial_{t}
; (ii) solving the extension problem for such nonlocal operators.

•

In Section
3.6
we define the fractional heat operator
(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
through the evolutive heat semigroup and we show some of its basic properties, similarly to what we have done in Section
3.3
.

•

In Section
3.7
we solve the extension problem for the fractional heat operator. In Definition
3.7.1
we define the Poisson kernel for the extension problem, and this allows us to define the following function

U(x,y,t):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau.

To reach our goal, i.e. to solve the extension problem, we make the crucial observation that
U
can be written in the following form using the evolutive heat semigroup
P_{\tau}^{H}

U(x,y,t):=\frac{1}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}y^{1-a}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)\,d\tau.

4
we first want to define and then solve the extension problem for the fractional powers of higher order of
\partial_{t}-\Delta
. In particular we have

In Section
4.1
we want to introduce the fractional operators of higher order considered in the previous chapters. In order to do this, we use the Balakrishnan formulation, which permits to give a natural generalization to higher order in both cases.

•

Our goal in Section
4.2
is to give the statement of the extension problem of higher order and prove it in Subsection
4.2.1

4.

In the concluding remarks
5
we comment on how to generalize the results obtained in Chapter
4
, and specifically in a forthcoming work we are going to solve the extension problem of higher order for the following class of operators

\mathscr{K}u:=\text{tr}\,(Q\nabla^{2}u)+\braket{BX,\nabla u}-\partial_{t}u,

where
Q
and
B
are
N\times N
matrices with real, constant coefficients, with
Q\geq 0,Q=Q^{*}
.

Chapter 2
Fractional Laplacean

2.1
The fractional Laplacean

In this section we introduce the M. Riesz’ fractional Laplacean
(-\Delta)^{s}
, with
0<s<1
. Our first goal is to give a definition of this nonlocal operator.

Our initial observation is the following simple calculus lemma which could be used to provide a probabilistic interpretation oh the classical Laplacean on the real line.

Lemma 2.1.1

Let
f\in C^{\,2}(a,b)
, then for every
x\in(a,b)
one has

-f\,^{\prime\prime}(x)=\lim_{y\to 0}\frac{2f(x)-f(x+y)-f(x-y)}{y^{2}}

The expression in the right-hand side in the equation in Lemma
2.1.1
is known as the symmetric difference quotient of order two. If we introduce the ”spherical” surface and ”solid” averaging operators

\mathscr{M}_{y}\,f(x)=\frac{f(x+y)+f(x-y)}{2},\hskip 8.50012pt\mathscr{A}_{y}\,f(x)=\frac{1}{2y}\int_{x-y}^{x+y}f(t)\,dt,

then we can reformulate the conclusion in Lemma
2.1.1
as follows:

-f\,^{\prime\prime}(x)=2\lim_{y\to 0}\frac{f(x)-\mathscr{M}_{y}\,f(x)}{y^{2}}=6\lim_{y\to 0}\frac{f(x)-\mathscr{A}_{y}\,f(x)}{y^{2}}

where it is easily seen that the second equality follows from the first one and L’Hopital’s rule. The result that follows generalizes this observation to
n\geq 2
.

Proposition 2.1.2

Let
\Omega\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set. For any
f\in C^{2}(\Omega)
and
x\in\Omega
we have

-\Delta f(x)=2n\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{f(x)-\mathscr{M}_{r}\,f(x)}{r^{2}}=2(n+2)\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{f(x)-\mathscr{A}_{r}\,f(x)}{r^{2}}

(2.1)

where
\Delta f
is the operator of Laplace.

In the equation (
2.1
) we have indicated with

\mathscr{M}_{r}u(x)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{n-1}r^{n-1}}\int_{S(x,r)}u(y)\,d\sigma(y),\hskip 8.50012pt\mathscr{A}_{r}u(x)=\frac{1}{\omega_{n}r^{n}}\int_{B(x,r)}u(y)\,dy,

(2.2)

the spherical surface and solid mean-value operators. Here,
B(x,r)=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\lvert y-x\rvert<r\},\,\,S(x,r)=\partial B(x,r)
,
d\sigma
is the
(n-1)
-Lebesgue measure on
S(x,r)
, and the numbers
\sigma_{n-1}
and
\omega_{n}
respectively represent the measure of the unit sphere and that of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Before proceeding, and in preparation for the central definition of this section, let us observe that it is easy to recognize that we can write the second identity in (
2.1
) in the more suggestive fashion:

-\Delta u(x)=c(n)\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{r^{n+2}}\mathds{1}_{B(0,r)}(y)\,dy,

(2.3)

where we have denoted by
\mathds{1}_{E}
the indicator function of a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In the applied sciences it is of great importance to be able to consider fractional derivatives of functions. There exist many different definitions of such operations, but perhaps the most prominent one is based on the notion of (Marcel) Riesz’ potential of a function. To motivate such operation let us assume that
n\geq 3
, and recall that in mathematical physics the Newtonian potential of a function
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is given by

I_{2}(f)(x)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}\Gamma\left(\frac{n-2}{2}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{f(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{n-2}}\,dy,

Now, one recognizes that the convolution kernel
\frac{1}{4\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}\Gamma\left(\frac{n-2}{2}\right)\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert^{n-2}}
in the definition of
I_{2}(f)
is just the fundamental solution

E(x)=\frac{1}{(n-2)\sigma_{n-1}}\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert^{n-2}}

of
-\Delta
. With this observation in mind, we recall the well-known identity of Gauss-Green that says that for any
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
one has

I_{2}(-\Delta f)=f.

In other words, the Newtonian potential is the inverse of
-\Delta
. This important observation leads to the introduction of M. Riesz’ generalization of the Newtonian potential.

Definition 2.1.3
(Riesz’ potentials)

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
0<\alpha<n
. The Riesz potential of order
\alpha
is the operator whose action on a function
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is given by

I_{\alpha}(f)(x)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-\alpha}{2}\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}2^{\alpha}\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{f(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{n-\alpha}}\,dy.

The important reason behind the normalization constant is that such constant is chosen to guarantee the validity of the following crucial result, a kind of fractional fundamental theorem of calculus, stating that for any
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
one has in
\mathscr{S}\,^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

I_{\alpha}(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f=(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}I_{\alpha}f.

(2.4)

Of course (
2.4
) makes no sense unless we say what we mean by the fractional operator
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
. The most natural way to introduce it is by defining the action of
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
on the Fourier transform side by the equation

\mathscr{F}((-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u)=(2\pi\lvert\cdot\rvert)^{\alpha}\mathscr{F}(u),\hskip 8.50012ptu\in\mathscr{S}\,^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(2.5)

The equation (
2.4
) shows that
I_{\alpha}
inverts the fractional powers of the Laplacean, i.e.,

I_{\alpha}=(-\Delta)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}},\hskip 8.50012pt0<\alpha<n.

(2.6)

For this reason
I_{\alpha}
is also called the
fractional integration operator
of order
\alpha
.

Since our focus in this section is the fractional Laplacean
(-\Delta)^{s}
in the range
0<s<1
, we will henceforth let
s=\alpha/2
in the above formulas. Although we have formally introduced such operator in the equation (
2.5
) above, such definition has a major drawback:it is not easy understand a given function (or a distribution) by prescribing its Fourier transform. It is for this reason that we begin our story introducing a different pointwise definition of the fractional Laplacean that is more directly connected to the symmetric difference quotient of order two in the opening calculus Lemma
2.1.1
, and with (
2.3
).

Definition 2.1.4

Let
0<s<1
. The fractional Laplacean of a function
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the nonlocal operator in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined by the expression

(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy,

(2.7)

where
\gamma(n,s)>0
is a suitable normalization constant that will be given implicitly in the future.

It is obvious that (
2.7
) defines a linear operator since for any
u,v\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
c\in\mathbb{R}
one has

(-\Delta)^{s}(u+v)=(-\Delta)^{s}u+(-\Delta)^{s}v,\hskip 8.50012pt(-\Delta)^{s}(cu)=c(-\Delta)^{s}u.

It is also important to observe that the integral in the right-hand side of (
2.7
) is convergent. To see this, it suffices to write

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy&=\int_{\lvert y\rvert\leq 1}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\\
&+\int_{\lvert y\rvert>1}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy.\end{split}

Taylor’s formula for
C^{2}
functions gives for
\lvert y\rvert\leq 1

2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)=-\Braket{\nabla^{2}u(x)y,y}+o(\lvert y\rvert^{2}),

where we have indicated with
\nabla^{2}u
the Hessian matrix of
u
. Therefore,

\left\lvert\int_{\lvert y\rvert\leq 1}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\right\rvert\leq C\int_{\lvert y\rvert\leq 1}\frac{dy}{\lvert y\rvert^{n-2(1-s)}}<\infty,

since
0<s<1
. On the other hand, keeping in mind that
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
implies in particular that
u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have

\left\lvert\int_{\lvert y\rvert>1}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\right\rvert\leq 4\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\int_{\lvert y\rvert>1}\frac{dy}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}<\infty.

We have seen that for every
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
definition
2.1.4
provides a well-defined function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Two basic operations in analysis are the Euclidean translations and dilations

\tau_{h}f(x)=f(x+h),\hskip 8.50012pth\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\hskip 17.00024pt\delta_{\lambda}f(x)=f(\lambda x),\hskip 8.50012pt\lambda>0.

The next result clarifies the interplay of
(-\Delta)^{s}
with them.

Lemma 2.1.5

For every function
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we have for every
h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(-\Delta)^{s}(\tau_{h}u)=\tau_{h}((-\Delta)^{s}u),

and every
\lambda>0

(-\Delta)^{s}(\delta_{\lambda}u)=\lambda^{2s}\delta_{\lambda}((-\Delta)^{s}u).

A fundamental property of the Laplacean
\Delta
is its invariance with respect to the action of the orthogonal group
\mathbb{O}(n)
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This means that if
u
is a function in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then for every
T\in\mathbb{O}(n)
one has
\Delta(u\circ T)=\Delta u\circ T
. The following lemma shows that
(-\Delta)^{s}
enjoys the same property.

Lemma 2.1.6

Let
u(x)=f(\lvert x\rvert)
be a function with spherical symmetry in
C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then, also
(-\Delta)^{s}u
has spherical symmetry.

Proof:

This follows in a simple way from (
2.7
). In order to prove that
(-\Delta)^{s}u
is spherically symmetric we need to show that for every
T\in\mathbb{O}(n)
and every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
one has

(-\Delta)^{s}u(Tx)=(-\Delta)^{s}u(x).

We have

\begin{split}(-\Delta)^{s}u(Tx)&=\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2f(\lvert Tx\rvert)-f(\lvert Tx+y\rvert)-f(\lvert Tx-y\rvert)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\\
&=\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2f(\lvert x\rvert)-f(\lvert x+T^{t}y\rvert)-f(\lvert x-T^{t}y\rvert)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy.\end{split}

If we make the change of variable
z=T^{t}y
, we conclude

\begin{split}(-\Delta)^{s}u(Tx)&=\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2f(\lvert x\rvert)-f(\lvert x+z\rvert)-f(\lvert x-z\rvert)}{\left\lvert Tz\right\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dz\\
&=\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2f(\lvert x\rvert)-f(\lvert x+z\rvert)-f(\lvert x-z\rvert)}{\left\lvert z\right\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dz\\
&=(-\Delta)^{s}u(x),\end{split}

And we are done.

\square

Before proceeding we note the following alternative expression for
(-\Delta)^{s}
that is at times quite useful in the computations.

Proposition 2.1.7

For any
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
one has

(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=\gamma(n,s)\,\text{PV}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{u(x)-u(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy,

(2.8)

where now the integral is taken according to Cauchy’s principal value sense

\text{PV}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{u(x)-u(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\lvert y-x\rvert>\varepsilon}\frac{u(x)-u(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy

Proof:

The expression (
2.8
) follows directly from (
2.7
) above as follows

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\lvert y\rvert>\varepsilon}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\lvert y\rvert>\varepsilon}\frac{u(x)-u(x+y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy+\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\lvert y\rvert>\varepsilon}\frac{u(x)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\\
&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\lvert y\rvert>\varepsilon}\frac{u(x)-u(y)}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy.\end{split}

However, it is now necessary to take the principal value of the integral since we have eliminated the cancellation of the linear terms in the symmetric difference of order two, and
u(x)-u(y)
in only
O(\lvert x-y\rvert)
. Thus, the smoothness of
u
no longer guarantees the local integrability, unless we are in the regime
0<s<1/2
.

\square

Before proceeding we recall that
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the space
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
endowed with the metric topology

d(f,g)=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}2^{-p}\frac{\lVert f-g\rVert_{p}}{1+\lVert f-g\rVert_{p}},

generated by the countable family of norms

\lVert f\rVert_{p}=\sup_{\lvert\alpha\rvert\leq p}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}\lvert\partial^{\alpha}f(x)\rvert,\hskip 8.50012ptp\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}.

(2.9)

Now, we can prove that
(-\Delta)^{s}u
suitably decays at infinity:

Proposition 2.1.8

Let
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\lvert x\rvert>1
, we have

\lvert(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)\rvert\leq C_{u,n,s}\lvert x\rvert^{-(x+2s)},

where with
\lVert x\rVert_{p}
as in (
2.9
), we have let

C_{u,n,s}=C_{n,s}(\lVert u\rVert_{n+2}+\lVert u\rVert_{n}+\lVert u\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}).

Proof:

To see this we write

\begin{split}(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)&=\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\lvert y\rvert<\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\\
&+\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\lvert y\rvert\geq\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy.\end{split}

Taylor’s formula gives

2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)=-\frac{1}{2}\Braket{\nabla^{2}u(y^{*})y,y}-\frac{1}{2}\Braket{\nabla^{2}u(y^{**})y,y},

where
y^{*}=x+yt^{*}
,
y^{**}=x+yt^{**}
, for
t^{*},t^{**}\in[0,1]
. We now observe that on the set where
\lvert y\rvert<\lvert x\rvert/2
we have by the triangle inequality

\lvert x\rvert<2\lvert y^{*}\rvert\hskip 17.00024pt\lvert x\rvert<2\lvert y^{**}\rvert.

(2.10)

Using (
2.10
) and the definition (
2.9
) of the norm
\lvert u\rvert_{n+2}
in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we find

\begin{split}&\left\lvert\int_{\lvert y\rvert<\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\lvert y\rvert<\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{\lvert\nabla^{2}u(y^{*})\rvert+\lvert\nabla^{2}u(y^{**})\rvert}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\lvert y\rvert^{2}\,dy\\
&\leq C\lVert u\rVert_{n+2}\left(\int_{\lvert y\rvert<\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}{(1+\lvert y^{*}\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n+2}{2}}\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}},dy+\int_{\lvert y\rvert<\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}{(1+\lvert y^{**}\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n+2}{2}}\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}},dy\right)\\
&\leq C\lvert x\rvert^{-n-2}\lVert u\rVert_{n+2}\int_{\lvert y\rvert<\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{dy}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s-2}}=C\lvert x\rvert^{-n-2}\lVert u\rVert_{n+2}\lvert x\rvert^{2-2s}=C\lVert u\rVert_{n+2}\lvert x\rvert^{-(x+2s)},\end{split}

where
C=C_{n,s}>0
.

Next, we estimate

\begin{split}&\left\lvert\int_{\lvert y\rvert\geq\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\right\rvert\leq 2\int_{\lvert y\rvert\geq\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{\lvert u(x+y)-u(x)\rvert}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\\
&\leq 2\int_{\lvert y\rvert\geq\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{\lvert u(x+y)+u(x)\rvert}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy.\end{split}

We have

\begin{split}&\int_{\lvert y\rvert>\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{\lvert u(x)\rvert}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}((1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n}{2}}\lvert u(x)\rvert)\int_{\lvert y\rvert\geq\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{dy}{(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n}{2}}\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\\
&\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}((1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n}{2}}\lvert x\rvert^{-n}\int_{\lvert y\rvert\geq\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{dy}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\leq\frac{C\lVert u\rVert_{n}}{\lvert x\rvert^{n+2s}},\end{split}

where
C=C_{n,s}>0
. Finally, we have trivially

\int_{\lvert y\rvert\geq\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\frac{\lvert u(x+y)\rvert}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\leq\frac{2^{n+2s}}{\lvert x\rvert^{n+2s}}\int_{\lvert y\rvert\geq\frac{\lvert x\rvert}{2}}\lvert u(x+y)\rvert\,dy\leq\frac{2^{n+2s}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}}{\lvert x\rvert^{n+2s}}.

This completes the proof.

\square

Proposition
2.1.8
has the following non trivial consequence.

Corollary 2.1.9

Let
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.Then,
(-\Delta)^{s}u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

The estimate in Proposition
2.1.8
can be written

-C_{u,n,s}\lvert x\rvert^{-(n+2s)}\leq-(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)\leq C_{u,n,s}\lvert x\rvert^{-(n+2s)}.

Let us notice that on a nonnegative bump function the estimate from below can be made stronger, a fact that reflects the nonlocal character of
(-\Delta)^{s}
. Suppose for instance that
u\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
0\leq u\leq 1
,
u\equiv 1
on
B(0,1)
and
\text{supp }u\subset\bar{B}(0,2)
. Then, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B(0,3)
one has from (
2.7
)

\begin{split}-(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)&=\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{u(x+y)+u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\\
&\geq\gamma(n,s)\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{dz}{\lvert x-z\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dz.\end{split}

Since
\lvert x-z\rvert\geq 2
, for
\lvert z\rvert\leq 1
we infer
\lvert x\rvert\geq\lvert x-z\rvert-\lvert z\rvert\geq\lvert x-z\rvert-1\geq\lvert x-z\rvert/2
. This gives some
C(n,s)>0

-(-\Delta)^{s}u\geq C(n,s)\lvert x\rvert^{-(n+2s)}>0,

which shows that
(-\Delta)^{s}u
needs not to vanish even far away from the support of
u
. This is clearly impossible for local operators
P(x,\partial_{x})
, for which one has the obvious property supp
P(x,\partial_{x})u\subset\text{supp }u
.

2.2
A brief interlude about very classical stuff

To proceed with the analysis of the nonlocal operator
(-\Delta)^{s}
we will need some basic properties of two important, and deeply interconnected, protagonist of classical analysis: the Fourier transform and Bessel functions. Since they both play a pervasive role in these thesis, as a help to the reader in this section we recall their definition along with some elementary facts. Before we do that, however, we introduce the ever present Euler’s gamma function (see e.g. chapter 1 in
16
):

\Gamma(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{x-1}e^{-t}\,dt\hskip 8.50012ptx>0.

The well-known identity
\Gamma(1/2)=\sqrt{\pi}
is simply a reformulation of the famous integral

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-x^{2}}\,dx=\sqrt{\pi}.

Of course,
\Gamma(z)
can be equally defined as holomorphic function for every
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\mathfrak{R}z>0
. It easy to check that for such
z
, one has

\Gamma(z+1)=z\Gamma(z).

(2.11)

This formula, and its iterations, can be used to meromorphically extend
\Gamma(z)
to the whole complex plane having simple poles at
z=-k
,
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, with residues
(-1)^{k}
. In particular, when
0<s<1
, one obtains from (
2.11
)

\Gamma(1-s)=-s\Gamma(-s).

(2.12)

Furthermore, one has the following basic relations:

\Gamma(z)\Gamma(1-z)=\frac{\pi}{\sin\pi z},

(2.13)

and

2^{2z-1}\Gamma(z)\Gamma\left(z+\frac{1}{2}\right)=\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(2z).

(2.14)

Stirling’s formula
provides the asymptotic behavior of the gamma function for large positive values of its argument

\Gamma(x)=\sqrt{2\pi}x^{x-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-x}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)\right),\hskip 8.50012pt\text{as }x\to+\infty.

(2.15)

We close this brief prelude with a very classical formula which connects the gamma functions to the
(n-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure of the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and the
n
-dimensional volume of the unit ball

\sigma_{n-1}=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)},\hskip 17.00024pt\omega_{n}=\frac{\sigma_{n-1}}{n}=\frac{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+1\right)}.

(2.16)

One identity that we will use is the following

\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{-s-1}(1-e^{-u})\,du=\frac{1}{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{-s}e^{-u}\,du=\frac{\Gamma(1-s)}{s}.

(2.17)

Deeply connected with the gamma function is Euler’s
beta function
which for
x,y>0
is defined as follows

B(x,y)=2\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}(\cos\theta)^{2x-1}(\sin\theta)^{2y-1}\,d\theta.

(2.18)

It is an easy exercise to recognize that

B(x,y)=2\int_{0}^{1}(1-\tau^{2})^{x-1}\tau^{2y-1}\,d\tau=\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{x-1}s^{y-1}\,ds.

(2.19)

The link between the beta and the gamma function is expressed by the following equation

B(x,y)=\frac{\Gamma(x)\Gamma(y)}{\Gamma(x+y)},

(2.20)

see e.g. (1.5.6) on p. 14 in
16
. A useful integral which is expressed in terms of the beta, or gamma function is contained in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.2.1

Let
b>-n
and
a>n+b
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{b}}{(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{a}{2}}}\,dx=\frac{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{b+n}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{a-b-n}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{a+b}{2}\right)}.

(2.21)

In particular, if
b=0
and
a=n+1
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{dx}{(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}=\frac{\pi^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}\right)}.

(2.22)

Proof:

Let us observe preliminarily that the assumption
b>-n
serves to guarantee that the integrand belongs to
L_{\text{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, whereas it is in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
if and only if
a-b>n
. Under these hypothesis we have

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{b}}{(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{a}{2}}}\,dx=\sigma_{n-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{b+n-1}}{(1+r^{2})^{\frac{a}{2}}}\\
&=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{(\tan\xi)^{b+n-1}}{(1+\tan^{2}\xi)^{\frac{a-2}{2}}}\,d\xi=\sigma_{n-1}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}(\sin\xi)^{b+n-1}(\cos\xi)^{a-b-n-1}\,d\xi\\
&=\frac{\sigma_{n-1}}{2}B\left(\frac{b+n}{2},\frac{a-b-n}{2}\right),\end{split}

If we now apply formulas (
2.16
) and (
2.20
) we obtain (
2.21
). To obtain (
2.22
) it suffices to keep in mind that
\Gamma(1/2)=\sqrt{\pi}
.

\square

We are ready to introduce the queen of classical analysis: given a function
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we define its Fourier transform as

\mathscr{F}(u)(\xi)=\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{ 375.66913pt}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-2\pi\Braket{\xi,x}}u(x)\,dx.

We notice that the normalization that we have adopted in the above definition is the one which makes
\mathscr{F}
an isometry of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
onto itself, see
21
. We recall next some of the basic properties of
\mathscr{F}
. If
\tau_{h}u(x)=u(x+h)
and
\delta_{\lambda}u(x)=u(\lambda x)
are the translation and dilation operators in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then we have

\hbox{\hskip (2.23)

and

\hbox{\hskip hoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}\left(\frac{\xi}{\lambda}\right).

(2.24)

The Fourier transform is also invariant under the action of the orthogonal group
\mathbb{O}(n)
. We have in fact for every
T\in\mathbb{O}(n)

\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-9.53925pt\hbox{$u\circ 375.66913pt}=\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{ 375.66913pt}\circ T.

(2.25)

Formula (
2.25
) says that the Fourier transform of a spherically symmetric function is spherically symmetric as well.

Another crucial property is the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma:

u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\Longrightarrow\lvert\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{ 375.66913pt}(\xi)\rvert\to 0\text{ as }\lvert\xi\rvert\to\infty.

(2.26)

This result has important consequences when combined with the following two formulas. Let
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be such that for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
also
\partial^{\alpha}u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then,

\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi)=(2\pi i)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert}\xi^{\alpha}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{ 375.66913pt}(\xi).

(2.27)

In particular, (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) give:
\lvert\xi^{\alpha}\rvert\lvert\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{ 375.66913pt}(\xi)\rvert\to 0
as
\lvert\xi\rvert\to\infty
. Furthermore, if
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is such that for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
one has

, then,

\partial^{\alpha}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{ 375.66913pt}(\xi)=(-2\pi i)^{\lvert\alpha\rvert}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-9.75415pt\hbox{$(\cdot)^{\alpha}u$}\hskip-9.75415pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{8.59999pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi).

(2.28)

In particular, (
2.26
) and (
2.28
) imply that:
\partial^{\alpha}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\lvert\partial^{\alpha}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi)\rvert\to 0
as
\lvert\xi\rvert\to\infty
.

Combining these observations one derives one of the central properties of
\mathscr{F}
: it maps continuously
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
onto itself and is an isomorphism. Its inverse is also continuous, and is given by Fourier inversion formula

\mathscr{F}^{-1}(u)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{2\pi\Braket{\xi,x}}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi)\,d\xi.

We next introduce the second main character of this section: the Bessel functions. The book
16
provides a rewarding account of this beautiful classical subject.

Definition 2.2.2

For every
v\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathfrak{R}v>-\frac{1}{2}
we define the Bessel function of the first kind and of complex order
v
by the formula

J_{v}(z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(v+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{z}{2}\right)^{v}\int_{-1}^{1}e^{izt}(1-t^{2})^{\frac{2v-1}{2}}\,dt,

(2.29)

where
\Gamma(x)
denotes the Euler gamma function.

The function
J_{v}(z)
in (
2.29
) derives its name from the fact that it solves the linear ordinary differential equation known as
Bessel equation of order

v

z^{2}\frac{d^{2}J}{dz^{2}}+z\frac{dJ}{dz}+(z^{2}-v^{2})J=0.

(2.30)

An expression of
J_{v}
as a power series for an arbitrary value of
v\in\mathbb{C}
is provided by

J_{v}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\frac{(z/2)^{v+2k}}{\Gamma(k+1)\Gamma(k+v+1)},\hskip 8.50012pt\lvert z\rvert<\infty,\,\,\lvert\arg(z)\rvert<\pi,

(2.31)

see e.g. (5.3.2) on p. 102 in
16
. When
v\not\in\mathbb{Z}
, another linearly independent solution of (
2.30
) is provided by the function
J_{-v}(z)
. When
z\in\mathbb{Z}
the two functions
J_{v}
and
J_{-v}
are linearly dependent, and in order to find a second solution linearly independent from
J_{v}
one has to proceed differently.

The observation that follows is very important in most concrete applications of the theory. Suppose that
\varphi(z)
be a solution to the Bessel equation (
2.30
), and consider the function defined by the transformation

u(y)=y^{\alpha}\varphi(\beta y^{\gamma}).

(2.32)

Then, one easily verifies that
u(y)
satisfies the
generalized Bessel equation

y^{2}u^{\prime\prime}(y)+(1-2\alpha)yu^{\prime}(y)+[\beta^{2}\gamma^{2}y^{2\gamma}+(\alpha^{2}-v^{2}\gamma^{2})]u(y)=0.

(2.33)

Returning to Definition
2.2.2
, from (
2.29
) and (
2.19
) we immediately find

z^{-v}J_{v}(z)\xrightarrow[z\to 0]{}\frac{2^{-v+1}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(v+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s^{2})^{\frac{2v-1}{2}}\,ds=\frac{2^{-v+1}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(v+\frac{1}{2}\right)}B\left(v+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right).

From this asymptotic relation and (
2.20
) one obtains

J_{v}(z)\cong\frac{2^{-v}}{\Gamma(v+1)}z^{v},\hskip 8.50012pt\text{ as }z\to 0.

(2.34)

Unlike the simple expression of the asymptotic of
J_{v}(z)
as
z\to 0
, the behavior at infinity of
J_{v}(z)
is more delicate to come by. We have the following result, see (5.11.6) on p. 122 in
16
.

Lemma 2.2.3

Let
\mathfrak{R}v>-\frac{1}{2}
. One has

\begin{split}J_{v}(z)=&\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi z}}\cos\left(z-\frac{\pi v}{2}-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)+O(z^{-\frac{3}{2}})\\
&\text{as }\lvert z\rvert\to\infty,\hskip 8.50012pt-\pi+\delta<\arg z<\pi-\delta.\end{split}

(2.35)

In particular,

J_{v}(z)=O(z^{-\frac{1}{2}}),\hskip 8.50012pt\text{as }z\to\infty,\,\,z\geq 0.

(2.36)

Along with the Bessel equation (
2.30
), in Sections
2.4
and
2.5
below we will need the
modified Bessel equation
of order
v\in\mathbb{C}
,

z^{2}\frac{d^{2}\varphi}{dz^{2}}+z\frac{d\varphi}{dz}-(z^{2}+v^{2})\varphi=0.

(2.37)

Two linearly independent solutions of (
2.37
) are the modified Bessel function of the first kind,

I_{v}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(z/2)^{v+2k}}{\Gamma(k+1)\Gamma(k+v+1)},\hskip 8.50012pt\lvert z\rvert<\infty,\,\,\lvert\arg(z)\rvert<\pi,

(2.38)

and the modified Bessel function of the third kind, or Macdonald function, which for order
v\not=0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\dots
, is given by

K_{v}(z)=\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{I_{-v}(z)-I_{v}(z)}{\sin\pi v},\hskip 8.50012pt\lvert\arg(z)\rvert<\pi.

(2.39)

Notice that
K_{v}(z)=K_{-v}(z)
.

It easy to verify that if
\varphi(z)
is a solution to the modified Bessel equation (
2.37
), then the function defined by the transformation (
2.32
) satisfies the
generalized modified Bessel equation

y^{2}u^{\prime\prime}(y)+(1-2\alpha)yu^{\prime}(y)+[(\alpha^{2}-v^{2}\gamma^{2})-\beta^{2}\gamma^{2}y^{2\gamma}]u(y)=0.

(2.40)

As we have stated in the opening of this section the Fourier transform and the Bessel functions are deeply connected. One important instance of this link is the following result which provides a deeper meaning to the invariance of the Fourier transform with respect to the action of the orthogonal group
\mathbb{O}(n)
. We emphasize that the presence of Bessel functions in Theorem
2.2.4
below underscores the interplay between curvature (that of the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
) and Fourier analysis. For the following result we refer to Theorem 40 on p. 69 in
4
.

Theorem 2.2.4
(Fourier-Bessel representation)

Let
u(x)=f(\lvert x\rvert)
, and suppose that

t\to t^{\frac{n}{2}}f(t)J_{\frac{n}{2}-1}(t)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}),

where we have denoted by
J_{\frac{n}{2}-1}
the Bessel function of order
v=\frac{n}{2}-1
defined by (
2.29
). Then,

\hbox{\hskip 367.00667pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-367.00667pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{43315241}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-367.00667pt\hskip 367.00667pt}(\xi)=2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert^{-\frac{n}{2}+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{n}{2}}f(t)J_{\frac{n}{2}-1}(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert t)\,dt.

To check the integrability assumption in Theorem
2.2.4
we can use the above given asymptotic (
2.34
) and (
2.36
) for the Bessel function
J_{v}
.

Another family of special functions that will be needed in this paper are the so-called
hypergeometric functions
. In order to introduce them we recall the definition of the Pochammer’s symbols

\alpha_{0}=1,\hskip 8.50012pt\alpha_{k}:=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+k)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}=\alpha(\alpha+1)\dots(\alpha+k-1),\hskip 8.50012ptk\in\mathbb{N}.

Notice that since, as we have said,the gamma function has a pole in
z=0
, we have

0_{k}=\begin{cases}1\hskip 8.50012pt\text{ if }\hskip 8.50012ptk=0\\
0\hskip 8.50012pt\text{ for }\,\,k\geq 0.\end{cases}

Definition 2.2.5

Let
p,q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be such that
p\leq q+1
, and let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p}
and
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{q}
be give parameters such that
-\beta_{j}\not\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
for
j=1,\dots,q
. Given a nummber
z\in\mathbb{C}
, the power series

{}_{p}F_{q}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p};\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{q};z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\alpha_{1})_{k}\dots(\alpha_{p})_{k}}{(\beta_{1})_{k}\dots(\beta_{q})_{k}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}

is called the generalized hypergeometric function. When
p=2
and
q=1
, then the function
{}_{2}F_{1}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2};\beta_{1};z)
is the Gauss’ hypergeometric function, and it is usually denoted by
F(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2};\beta_{1};z)
.

We have

F(\alpha,0;\beta;z)=F(0,\alpha;\beta;z)=1,

(2.41)

and (see also p. 275 in
16
)

F(\alpha,\beta;\beta;-z)={}_{1}F_{0}(\alpha;-z)=(1+z)^{-\alpha}.

(2.42)

It also interesting to observe that the hypergeometric function
{}_{0}F_{1}
is in essence a Bessel function, up to powers and rescaling. One has in fact form (
2.38
) and Definition
2.2.5
,

I_{v}(z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(v+1)}\left(\frac{z}{2}\right)^{v}{}_{0}F_{1}(v+1;(z/2)^{2}).

(2.43)

2.3
Fourier, Bessel and fractional Laplacean

After our brief interlude on the Fourier transform and Bessel functions, we now return to the main protagonist of this chapter.

Proposition 2.3.1
(Pseudodifferential nature of
(-\Delta)^{s}
)

Let
\gamma(n,s)>0
be the number identified by the following formula

\gamma(n,s)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{1-\cos(z_{n})}{\lvert z\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dz=1.

(2.44)

Then, for any
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we have

\hbox{\hskip 367.00667pt\hskip-13.58717pt\hbox{$(-\Delta)^{s}u$}\hskip-13.58717pt\hskip-367.00667pt\raisebox{8.59999pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{43315241}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathch 367.00667pt}(\xi)=(2\pi\lvert xi\rvert)^{2s}\hbox{\hskip 367.00667pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-367.00667pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{43315241}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-367.00667pt\hskip 367.00667pt}(\xi).

(2.45)

Proof:

Let us observe that in view of Corollary (
2.1.9
) we know that
(-\Delta)^{s}u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and thus we can take its Fourier transform in the sense of
L^{1}
. Having said this, if denote by
\tau_{h}u(x)=u(x+h)
the translation operator in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we can rewrite (
2.7
) in the following way

(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2u(x)-\tau_{y}(x)-\tau_{-y}(x)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy.

(2.46)

Using (
2.23
) we easily find

\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi)=\gamma(n,s)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{1-\cos(2\pi\Braket{\xi,y})}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy\right)\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{
