mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi)=(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert)^{2s}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-8.73915pt\hbox{$E_{s,y}$}\hskip-8.73915pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{8.06665pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi).

(2.75)

We now use (
2.63
) in Lemma
2.4.5
. Inserting such equation in (
2.75
) we obtain

\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-8.68013pt\hbox{$F_{s,y}$}\hskip-8.68013pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{8.06665pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi)=(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert)^{2s}\frac{y^{s}}{2^{2s-1}\pi^{s}\Gamma(s)}\lvert\xi\rvert^{-s}K_{s}(2\pi y\lvert\xi\rvert)=\frac{2y^{s}\pi^{s}}{\Gamma(s)}\lvert\xi\rvert^{s}K_{s}(2\pi y\lvert\xi\rvert).

(2.76)

Using Theorem
2.2.4
we find from (
2.76
)

F_{s,y}(x)=\frac{4y^{s}\pi^{s+1}}{\Gamma(s)}\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert^{\frac{n}{2}-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{n}{2}+s}K_{s}(2\pi yt)J_{\frac{n}{2}-1}(2\pi\lvert x\rvert t)\,dt.

(2.77)

If we now let

\lambda=-\frac{n}{2}-s,\hskip 8.50012pt\mu=s,\hskip 8.50012ptv=\frac{n}{2}-1,

then we can wirte the integral in the right-hand side of (
2.77
) in the form

\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-\lambda}K_{\mu}(at)J_{v}(bt)\,dt,

with

a=2\pi y,\hskip 8.50012ptb=2\pi\lvert x\rvert.

Under the assumption
v-\lambda+1>\lvert\mu\rvert
, that is presently equivalent to
n+s>s
, which is obviously true, we can appeal to formula 3. in 6.576 on p. 693 in
13
. Such formula states that

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}&t^{-\lambda}K_{\mu}(at)J_{v}(bt)\,dt=\\
&=\frac{b^{v}\Gamma\left(\frac{v-\lambda+\mu+1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{v-\lambda-\mu+1}{2}\right)}{2^{\lambda+1}a^{v-\lambda+1}\Gamma(1+v)}\,F\left(\frac{v-\lambda+\mu+1}{2},\frac{v-\lambda-\mu+1}{2};v+1;-\frac{b^{2}}{a^{2}}\right),\end{split}

(2.78)

where, we recall,
F(\alpha,\beta;\gamma;z)
indicates the hypergeometric function
{}_{2}F_{1}(\alpha,\beta;\gamma;z)
, see Definition
2.2.5
above. Since

\frac{v-\lambda+\mu+1}{2}=\frac{n}{2}+s,\hskip 8.50012pt\frac{v-\lambda-\mu+1}{2}=\frac{n}{2},

from (
2.77
) and (
2.78
) we obtain

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}&t^{\frac{n}{2}+s}K_{s}(2\pi yt)J_{\frac{n}{2}-1}(2\pi\lvert x\rvert t)\,dt=\\
&=\frac{(2\pi\lvert x\rvert)^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\Gamma(\frac{n}{2}+s)}{2^{-\frac{n}{2}-s+1}(2\pi y)^{n+s}}\,F\left(\frac{n}{2}+s,\frac{n}{2};\frac{n}{2};-\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{y^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(2.79)

We now apply (
2.42
) to find

F\left(\frac{n}{2}+s,\frac{n}{2};\frac{n}{2};-\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{y^{2}}\right)=\left(1+\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{y^{2}}\right)^{-\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}.

Inserting this information into (
2.79
) we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{n}{2}+s}K_{s}(2\pi yt)J_{\frac{n}{2}-1}(2\pi\lvert x\rvert t)\,dt=\frac{(2\pi\lvert x\rvert)^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\Gamma(\frac{n}{2}+s)}{2^{-\frac{n}{2}-s+1}(2\pi y)^{n+s}}\left(1+\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{y^{2}}\right)^{-\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}.

(2.80)

From (
2.77
) and (
2.80
) we finally conclude

F_{s,y}(x)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{y^{n}\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\left(1+\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{y^{2}}\right)^{-\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}=\frac{y^{2s}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}(y^{2}+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{-\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}.

This establishes (
2.74
), thus completing the proof.

\square

We are now ready to provide the

Proof:

[Proof of Theorem
2.4.4
]
Our objective is establishing

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}E_{s}(x)(-\Delta)^{s}\phi(x)\,dx=\phi(0),

(2.81)

for every test function
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We begin by observing that, since we are assuming that
n\geq 2
, we automatically have that
0<s<\frac{n}{2}
. For
y>0
we now consider the regularization
E_{s,y}
of the distribution
E_{s}
defined by (
2.60
) and (
2.61
) above. Notice that
E_{s,y}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and decays at
\infty
like
\lvert x\rvert^{-(n-2s)}
. Since for
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we know from Lemma
2.4.1
that
(-\Delta)^{s}\phi\in\mathscr{S}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it should be clear that Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}E_{s,y}(x)(-\Delta)^{s}\phi(x)\,dx\longrightarrow\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}E_{s}(x)(-\Delta)^{s}\phi(x)\,dx

as
y\to 0^{+}
. On the other hand, Lemma
2.3.3
(which continues to be valid in the present situation) gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}E_{s,y}(x)(-\Delta)^{s}\phi(x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(-\Delta)^{s}E_{s,y}(x)\phi(x)\,dx.

(2.82)

Therefore, in view of (
2.82
), in order to complete the proof it will suffice to show that as
y\to 0^{+}

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(-\Delta)^{s}E_{s,y}(x)\phi(x)\,dx\longrightarrow\phi(0).

(2.83)

To establish (
2.83
) we use (
2.74
) in Lemma
2.4.6
which gives

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}&(-\Delta)^{s}E_{s,y}(x)\phi(x)\,dx=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{y^{n}\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(1+\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{y^{2}}\right)^{-\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}\phi(x)\,dx\\
&=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(1+\lvert x^{\prime}\rvert^{2})^{-\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}\phi(yx^{\prime})\,dx^{\prime}\\
&\longrightarrow\phi(0)\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(1+\lvert x^{\prime}\rvert^{2})^{-\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}\,dx^{\prime},\end{split}

where in the last equality we have used Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem. To complete the proof of (
2.83
) it would be sufficient to prove that

\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(1+\lvert x^{\prime}\rvert^{2})^{-\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}\,dx^{\prime}=1.

(2.84)

Now, the validity of (
2.84
) follows from a straightforward application of Proposition
2.2.1
with the choice
a=n+2s
,
b=0
.

\square

2.5
Traces of Bessel processes

When dealing with nonlocal operators such as
(-\Delta)^{s}
a major difficulty is represented by the fact the they do not act on functions like differential operators do, but instead through nonlocal integral formulas such as (
2.7
). As a consequence, the rules of differentiation are not readily available. In this perspective it would be highly desirable to have some kind of procedure that allows to connect nonlocal problems to ones for which the rules of differential calculus are available. Exploring this connection is the principal objective of this section.

During the past decade there has been an explosion of interest in the analysis of nonlocal operators such as (
2.7
) in connection with various problems from the applied sciences, analysis and geometry. The majority of these developments has been motivated by the remarkable 2007 ”extension paper”
7
by Caffarelli and Silvestre. In that paper the authors introduced a method that allows to convert nonlocal problems in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
into ones that involve a certain (degenerate) differential operator in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1}
. Precisely, it was shown in
7
that if for a given
0<s<1
and
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
one considers the function
U(x,y)
that solves the following Dirichlet problem in the half-space
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1}
:

\begin{cases}L_{a}U(x,y)=\text{div}_{x,y}(y^{a}\nabla_{x,y}U)=0\hskip 8.50012ptx\in\mathbb{R}^{n},y>0,\\
U(x,0)=u(x),\end{cases}

(2.85)

where now
a=1-2s
, then one can recover
(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)
by the following ”trace” relation

-\frac{2^{2s-1}\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma(1-s)}\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}y^{1-2s}\frac{\partial U}{\partial y}(x,y)=(-\Delta)^{s}u(x).

(2.86)

Thus, remarkably, (
2.86
) provides yet another way of characterizing
(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)
as thw weighted
Dirichlet-to-Neumann
map of the extension problem (
2.85
).

One key observation is that the second order degenerate elliptic equation in (
2.85
) can also be written in nondivergence form in the following way

\begin{cases}-\Delta_{x}U=\mathscr{B}_{a}U,\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\,\,(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1}\\
U(x,0)=u(x),\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\,\,\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
U(x,y)\to 0,\text{ as }y\to\infty,\hskip 8.50012pt\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\end{cases}

(2.87)

where we have denoted by

\mathscr{B}_{a}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}+\frac{a}{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}

(2.88)

the generator of the Bessel semigroup on
(\mathbb{R}^{+},y^{a}\,dy)
.

Theorem 2.5.1

Let
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then, the solution
U
to the extension problem (
2.85
) is given by

U(x,y)=P_{s}(\cdot,y)\star u(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}P_{s}(x-z,y)u(z)\,dz,

(2.89)

where

P_{s}(x,y)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\frac{y^{2s}}{(y^{2}+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n+2s}{2}}}

(2.90)

is the Poisson kernel for the extension problem in the half-space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}
. For
U
as in (
2.89
) one has

(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=-\frac{2^{2s-1}\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma(1-s)}\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}y^{a}\frac{\partial U}{\partial y}(x,y).

(2.91)

Proof:

2.85
), written in the form (

). If we take a partial Fourier transform of the latter with respect to the variable

(2.93)

Comparing (
2.93
) with the generalized modified Bessel equation in (
2.40
) above we see that the former fits into the general form of the latter provided that

\alpha=s,\hskip 8.50012pt\gamma=1,\hskip 8.50012ptv=s,\hskip 8.50012pt\beta=2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert.

Thus, according to (
2.40
), two linearly independent solutions of (
2.93
) are given by

u_{1}(y)=y^{s}I_{s}(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert y),\hskip 17.00024ptu_{2}(y)=y^{s}K_{s}(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert y).

It ensues that, for every
\xi\not=0
, the general solution of (
2.92
) is given by

\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-3.30128pt\hbox{$U$}\hskip-3.30128pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{8.06665pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{ The condition
\hbox{\hskip (2.94)

Next, we use the condition

to fix the constant

. When

we have

\hbox{\hskip s}(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert)^{-s},

Now from formula (5.7.1) on p. 108 of
16
, we have as
z\to 0

I_{s}(z)\cong\frac{1}{\Gamma(s+1)}\left(\frac{z}{2}\right)^{s},\hskip 17.00024ptI_{-s}(z)\cong\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-s)}\left(\frac{z}{2}\right)^{-s}.

) above, we find that as

,

375.66913pt}(\xi)}{2^{s-1}\Gamma(s)}.

Substituting such value of
B
in (
2.94
), we finally obtain

\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-3.30128pt\hbox{$U$}\hskip-3.30128pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{8.06665pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{ 375.66913pt}(\xi,y)=\frac{(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert)^{s}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi)}{2^{s-1}\Gamma(s)}y^{s}K_{s}(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert y).

(2.95)

At this point we want to invert the Fourier transform in (
2.95
). In fact, it is clear from the latter equation that the function
U(x,y)
will be given by (
2.89
), with
P_{s}(x,y)
as in(
2.90
), if we can show that

\mathscr{F}_{\xi\to x}^{-1}\left(\frac{(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert)^{s}}{2^{s-1}\Gamma(s)}y^{s}K_{s}(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert y)\right)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\frac{y^{2s}}{(y^{2}+\lvert\xi\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n+2s}{2}}}.

(2.96)

In view of Theorem
2.2.4
, the latter identity is equivalent to

\frac{2^{2}\pi^{s+1}y^{s}}{\lvert x\rvert^{\frac{n}{2}-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{n}{2}+s}K_{s}(2\pi yt)J_{\frac{n}{2}-1}(2\pi\lvert x\rvert t)\,dt=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\frac{y^{2s}}{(y^{2}+\lvert\xi\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n+2s}{2}}}.

(2.97)

We are thus left with proving (
2.97
). Remarkably, this identity has been already been established in (
2.80
) above. Therefore, (
2.97
) does hold and, with it, (
2.89
) and (
2.90
) as well.

In order to complete the proof of the theorem we are thus left with establishing (
2.91
). With this objective in mind we note that in view of (
2.45
) in Proposition
2.3.1
, proving (
2.91
) is equivalent to showing

(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert)^{2s}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt}}{\partial y}(\xi,y).

(2.98)

Keeping in mind that
a=1-2s
, and usind the formula

K_{s}^{\prime}(z)=\frac{s}{z}K_{s}(z)-K_{s+1}(z)

(see (5.7.9) on p. 110 of

), we obtain

y^{a}\frac{\partial\hbox{\hskip ), we finally have

y^{a}\frac{\partial\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-3.30128pt\hbox{$U$}\hskip-3.30128pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{8.06665pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}}{\partial y}(\xi,y)=-\frac{(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert)^{s+1}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi)}{2^{s-1}\Gamma(s)}y^{1-s}K_{1-s}(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert y).

Now, as before, we have as
y\to 0^{+}
,

y^{1-s}K_{1-s}(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert y)\longrightarrow 2^{-s}\Gamma(1-s)(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert)^{s-1}.

We finally reach the conclusion that, as
y\to 0^{+}
,

y^{a}\frac{\partial\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-3.30128pt\hbox{$U$}\hskip-3.30128pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{8.06665pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}}{\partial y}(\xi,y)\longrightarrow-\frac{\Gamma(1-s)}{2^{2s-1}\Gamma(s)}(2\pi\lvert\xi\rvert)^{2s}\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.46068pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.46068pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{6.04443pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi).

This proves (
2.98
), thus completing the proof.

\square

Remark 2.5.2

Using Proposition
2.2.1
with the choice
b=0
and
a=n+2s
, it is easy to recognize from (
2.90
) that

\lVert P_{s}(\cdot,y)\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}P_{s}(x,y)\,dx=1,\hskip 17.00024pt\text{for every }y>0.

(2.99)

Remark 2.5.3

Notice that when
s=1/2
we have
a=1-2s=0
, and the extension operator
L_{a}
becomes the standard Laplacean
L_{a}=\Delta_{x}+\partial_{y}^{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. From formula (
2.90
) we obtain in such case

P_{\frac{1}{2}}(x,y)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\frac{y}{(y^{2}+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n+1}{2}}},

which is in fact the standard Poisson kernel for the upper half-space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}
.

Remark 2.5.4

If we compare the expression of the Poisson kernel in (
2.90
) with (
2.74
) in Lemma
2.4.6
, we conclude that, remarkably, we have shown that

P_{s}(x,y)=(-\Delta)^{s}E_{s,y}(x),

(2.100)

where for
y>0
the function
E_{s,y}=c(n,s)(y^{2}+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}
is the
y
-regularization of the fundamental solution of
(-\Delta)^{s}
. If we combine (
2.100
) with (
2.83
) above, we see that we can reformulate (
2.83
) as follows

\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}P_{s}(\cdot,y)=\delta\hskip 17.00024pt\text{in }\,\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

or, equivalently, for any
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}P_{s}(x,y)\phi(x)\,dx=\phi(0).

If we let
\tilde{\phi}(x)=\phi(-x)
, then we obtain from the latter limit relation

P_{s}(\cdot,y)\star\phi(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}P_{s}(z,y)\tau_{-x}\tilde{\phi}(z)\,dz\longrightarrow\tau_{-x}\tilde{\phi}(0)=\phi(x).

(2.101)

Remark 2.5.5
(Alternative proof of (
2.91
))

Usiin the property (
2.101
) of the Poisson kernel
P_{s}(x,y)
we can provide another ”short” proof of (
2.91
) along the following lines, see Section 3.1 in
7
. Let
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and consider the solution
U(x,y)=P_{s}(\cdot,y)\star u(x)
to the extension problem (
2.85
), see (
2.89
). Using (
2.99
) we can write

U(x,y)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{y^{2s}}{(y^{2}+\lvert x-z\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n+2s}{2}}}\,dz+u(x).

Differentiating both sides of this formula with respect to
y
and keeping in mind that
a=1-2s
, we obtain that as
y\to 0^{+}

y^{a}\frac{\partial U}{\partial y}(x,y)=2s\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{u(z)-u(x)}{(y^{2}+\lvert z-x\rvert^{2})^{\frac{n+2s}{2}}}\,dz+O(y^{2}).

Letting
y\to 0^{+}
and using Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, we thus find

\begin{split}\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}y^{a}\frac{\partial U}{\partial y}(x,y)&=2s\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\text{ PV }\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{u(z)-u(x)}{\lvert z-x\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dz\\
&=-2s\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+s\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma(s)}\gamma(n,s)^{-1}(-\Delta)^{s}u(x),\end{split}

where in the second equality we have used (
2.8
) above. If in the latter equation we now replace the expression (
2.52
) of the constant
\gamma(n,s)
, we reach the conclusion that (
2.91
) is valid.

\square

The Poisson kernel
P_{s}(x,y)
is of course a solution of
L_{a}P_{s}=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}
. What is instead not obvious is that the
y
-regularization
E_{s,y}
of the fundamental solution
E_{s}
of
(-\Delta)^{s}
introduced in (
2.62
) in Lemma
2.4.5
is also a solution of the extension operator
L_{a}
. It was shown in
7
that, up to a constant, such function is in fact the fundamental solution of
L_{a}
. The heuristic motivation behind this is that, with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{a+1}
, if
y=\lvert\eta\rvert
then the operator

y^{-a}L_{a}=\Delta_{x}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}+\frac{a}{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}

(2.102)

ca be thought of as the Laplacean in the fractional dimension
N=n+a+1
acting on functions
U(x,\lvert\eta\rvert)
. Such heuristic is confirmed by the following result.

Proposition 2.5.6

For
y\in\mathbb{R}
consider the function
G(x,y)=(\lvert x\rvert^{2}+y^{2})^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}
, see (
2.62
). Then, for every
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}
, with
a=1-2s
we have

L_{a}G(x,y)=0.

Proof:

It is convenient to use the expression of (
2.102
) on functions depending on
r=\lvert x\rvert
and
y

y^{-a}L_{a}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial r^{2}}+\frac{n-1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}+\frac{a}{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}.

Then, the proof becomes a simple computation. Abusing the notation we write

G(x,y)=G(r,y)=(r^{2}+y^{2})^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}.

We have

G_{r}=-(n+a-1)(r^{2}+y^{2})^{-\frac{n+a-1}{2}-1}r,

G_{rr}=(n+a-1)(r^{2}+y^{2})^{-\frac{n+a-1}{2}-2}((n+a)r^{2}-y^{2}).

This gives

G_{rr}+\frac{n-1}{r}G_{r}=(n+a-1)(r^{2}+y^{2})^{-\frac{n+a-1}{2}-2}((1+a)r^{2}-ny^{2}).

On the other hand, a similar computation gives

G_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}G_{y}=-(n+a-1)(r^{2}+y^{2})^{-\frac{n+a-1}{2}-2}((1+a)r^{2}-ny^{2}).

Adding the latter two equations gives the desired conclusion
L_{a}G=0
.

\square

Chapter 3
Fractional calculus

3.1
The heat semigroup

Given a set
X
, a
dynamical system
is a family
\{T(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
of mappings
T(t):X\to X
such that

•

T(t+s)=T(t)T(s)
for all
t,s\geq 0
,

•

T(0)=I_{X}
.

One can interpret
X
as the set of all states of a system,
t\in[0,\infty)
as time, and
T(t)
as the map describing the change of a state
x\in X
at time
t=0
into the state
T(t)x
at time
t>0
. When the state space
X
is a vector space and each
T(t)
is a linear operator on
X
, then
\{T(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
is called a (one-parameter)
semigroup
of operators. When
X
is a normed space, we say that it is a semigroup of
contractions
on
X
if for every
t\geq 0

\lVert T(t)x\rVert\leq\lVert x\rVert,\hskip 17.00024ptx\in X.

When the normed space
X
is a Banach space we say that a semigroup of bounded linear operators
\{T(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
on
X
is
strongly continuous
if for every
x\in X
its orbit map

t\longrightarrow T(t)x

is continuous from
[0,\infty)
into X. Strongly continuous semigroups are important because they represent a generalisation of the exponential function
t\to e^{tA}
of a matrix
A\in M_{n\times n}(\mathbb{C})
. Just as exponential functions provide a solutions of a scalar linear constant coefficient ordinary differential equations, strongly continuous semigroups provide solutions of linear constant coefficient ordinary differential equations in Banach spaces. Typically, such differential equations in Banach spaces arise from PDEs.

We begin our discussion by considering the ubiquitous Gaussian

K(x)=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{4}}.

Obviously,
K\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and we easily have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K(x)\,dx=1.

We next consider the following approximate identity associated with such kernel
K

G(x,t):=t^{-\frac{n}{2}}K\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}}\right)=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{4t}}\hskip 17.00024ptt>0.

(3.1)

The inquisitive reader might winder why we have scaled by
\sqrt{t}
and not just
t
. This is due to the fact that the function
G(x,t)
introduced in (
3.1
) is the fundamental solution of the heat operator
\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}
, and for such operator the correct scaling is provided by the non-homogeneous (parabolic) dilations
\lambda\to(\lambda x,\lambda^{2}t)
.

The next proposition contains an elementary but very important property of the function defined by (
3.1
).

Proposition 3.1.1
(Chapman-Kolmogorov equation)

For every
s,t>0
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
one has

G(x-y,t+s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-z,t)G(z-y,s)\,dz.

Proof:

We note that, by translation, it suffices to prove such identity when
y=0
. We thus need to show that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t,s>0
we have

G(x,t+s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-z,t)G(z,s)\,dz,

or equivalently

(4\pi(t+s))^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{4(t+s)}}=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}(4\pi s)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\left(\frac{\lvert x-z\rvert^{2}}{4t}+\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{2}}{4s}\right)}\,dz.

We now perform some elementary manipulations in the exponential in the integral in the right-hand side to find

\begin{split}\frac{\lvert x-z\rvert^{2}}{4t}+\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{2}}{4s}&=\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{4(t+s)}+\frac{1}{4t}\left(\frac{4s}{4(t+s)}\lvert x\rvert^{2}+\lvert z\rvert^{2}-2\braket{x,z}+\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{2}}{4s}4t\right)\\
&=\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{4(t+s)}+\left\lvert\left(\frac{4s}{4t4(t+s)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}x-\left(\frac{4(t+s)}{4t4s}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}z\right\rvert^{2}.\end{split}

This gives

\begin{split}(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}&(4\pi s)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\left(\frac{\lvert x-z\rvert^{2}}{4t}+\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{2}}{4s}\right)}\,dz\\
&=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}(4\pi s)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{4(t+s)}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\left\lvert\left(\frac{4s}{4t4(t+s)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}x-\left(\frac{4(t+s)}{4t4s}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}z\right\rvert^{2}}\,dz.\end{split}

The change of variable

z\longrightarrow\xi=\left(\frac{4s}{4t4(t+s)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}x-\left(\frac{4(t+s)}{4t4s}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}z,

for which we have

d\xi=\left(\frac{4(t+s)}{4t4s}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\,dz,

now gives

\begin{split}(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}&(4\pi s)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\left(\frac{\lvert x-z\rvert^{2}}{4t}+\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{2}}{4s}\right)}\,dz\\
&=\frac{\pi^{-\frac{n}{2}}}{(4(t+s))^{\frac{n}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}}\,d\xi=(4\pi(t+s))^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{4(t+s)}},\end{split}

which finally proves the desired conclusion.

\square

The next result expresses a fundamental property of the function
G(x,t)
.

Lemma 3.1.2

For every
x\in Rn
and
t>0
one has

\partial_{t}G(x,t)-\Delta G(x,t)=0.

Proof:

From the definition (
3.1
) it is immediate too verify that

\nabla G(x,t)=-\frac{x}{2t}G(x,t).

Then we find

\Delta G(x,t)=-\frac{1}{2t}\text{div }(G(\cdot,t)x)=-\frac{n}{2t}G(x,t)+\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{4t}G(x,t).

On the other hand, differentiating in
t
we easily find

\partial_{t}G(x,t)=-\frac{n}{2t}G(x,t)+\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}{4t}G(x,t).

The desired conclusion follows.

\square

For reasons that will become clear subsequently we now introduce a special notation for the convolution with
G(\cdot,t)

P_{t}f(x)=G(\cdot,t)\star f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-y,t)f(y)\,dy,

(3.2)

where
f
is a measurable function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for which the integral (
3.2
) makes sense. We can see that, as a linear operator,
P_{t}:L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
1\leq p\leq\infty
and moreover

\lVert P_{t}f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(3.3)

Furthermore, we have the following.

Proposition 3.1.3

Let
t,s>0
. For every
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we have

P_{t+s}f=P_{t}(P_{s}f).

Proof:

In view of (
3.3
) and Proposition
3.1.1
we have

\begin{split}P_{t+s}f(x)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-y,t+s)f(y)\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-z,t)G(z-y,s)\,dzf(y)\,dy\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-z,t)P_{s}f(z)\,dz=P_{t}(P_{s}f)(x).\end{split}

\square

Theorem 3.1.4

Let
1\leq p<\infty
. If
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we have

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\lVert P_{t}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0.

(3.4)

If instead
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then we have

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}P_{t}f(x)=f(x)

(3.5)

at every point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
of continuity for
f
.

As a consequence of Proposition
3.1.3
, (
3.3
) and Theorem
3.1.4
we obtain the following basic result.

Proposition 3.1.5

For every
1\leq p\leq\infty
the one-parameter family
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup of contractions on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The semigroup is strongly continuous when
1\leq p<\infty
.

The family of operators
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is called the
heat semigroup
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The name is justified by the fact that the function
u(x,t)=P_{t}f(x)
solves the Cauchy problem for the heat equation
\partial_{t}u-\Delta u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Denote the semigroup
P_{t}
with the symbol
e^{t\Delta}
. Clearly, if we formally let
t=0
we find
u(x,0)=f(x)
. Furthermore, by differentiating formally in
t
one has

u_{t}(x,t)=\Delta e^{t\Delta}f(x)=\Delta u(x,t),

(3.6)

therefore
u(x,t)=P_{t}f(x)
satisfies the heat equation
\partial_{t}u-\Delta u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Proposition 3.1.6

Given
f\in C(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the function
u(x,t)=P_{t}f(x)
is a solution of the Cauchy problem for the heat equation

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\Delta u=0\hskip 8.50012pt\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{+},\\
u(x,0)=f(x).\end{cases}

Another fact suggested by (
3.6
) is the commutation property
P_{t}\Delta=\Delta P_{t}
. The next proposition establishes this.

Proposition 3.1.7

For every
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
one has for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t>0

P_{t}(\Delta f)(x)=\Delta(P_{t}f)(x).

Moreover in general, if
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
one has

P_{t}(\partial^{\beta}f)(x)=\partial^{\beta}(P_{t}f)(x).

Proof:

Let
f\in C_{0}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and fix
R>0
so large that
\text{supp }f\subset B(0,R)
. Then

\begin{split}P_{t}(\Delta f)(x)&=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{B(0,R)}e^{-\frac{\lvert y-x\rvert^{2}}{4t}}\Delta_{y}f(y)\,dy\\
&=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{B(0,R)}\Delta_{y}\left(e^{-\frac{\lvert y-x\rvert^{2}}{4t}}\right)f(y)\,dy\\
&=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{B(0,R)}\Delta_{x}\left(e^{-\frac{\lvert y-x\rvert^{2}}{4t}}\right)f(y)\,dy\\
&=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\Delta_{x}\int_{B(0,R)}e^{-\frac{\lvert y-x\rvert^{2}}{4t}}f(y)\,dy\\
&=\Delta(P_{t}f)(x).\end{split}

\square

The following result is a simple but useful consequence of the commutation property in Proposition
3.1.7
.

Lemma 3.1.8

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
. Given any
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any
t\in[0,1]
we have

\lVert P_{t}f-f\rVert_{p}\leq\lVert\Delta f\rVert_{p}t.

Proof:

By proposition
3.1.6
and
3.1.7
we have for any
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

P_{t}f(x)-f(x)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{d}{d\tau}P_{\tau}f(x)\,d\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta P_{\tau}f(x)\,d\tau=\int_{0}^{t}P_{\tau}\Delta f(x)\,d\tau.

This gives for any
0\leq t\leq 1
,

\lVert P_{t}f-f\rVert_{p}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\lVert P_{\tau}\Delta f\rVert_{p}\,d\tau\leq\lVert\Delta f\rVert_{p}\int_{0}^{t}\,d\tau=\lVert\Delta f\rVert_{p}t.

where in the second inequality we have used (
3.3
).

\square

3.2
Ultracontractivity

We next establish a basic property of the semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

Proposition 3.2.1
(Ultracontractivity)

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t>0
we have

\lvert P_{t}f(x)\rvert\leq\frac{c(n,p)}{t^{\frac{n}{2p}}}\lVert f\rVert_{p}.

(3.7)

for a certain constant
c(n,p)>0
(when
p=1
one has
c(n,1)=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}
). In particular, for any
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
x\in Rn
one has

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lvert P_{t}f(x)\rvert=0.

(3.8)

Proof:

Applying Hölder’s inequality to (
3.2
) we find

\lvert P_{t}f(x)\rvert\leq\lVert f\rVert_{p}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-y,t)^{p^{\prime}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}=\lVert f\rVert_{p}(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\frac{p^{\prime}\lvert x-y\rvert^{2}}{4t}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}},

with
1/p+1/p^{\prime}=1
. By the change of variable
z=\sqrt{\frac{p^{\prime}}{4t}}(y-x)
, for which
dz=\left(\frac{p^{\prime}}{4t}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\,dy
, the desired conclusion immediately follows with
c(n,p)=\left(\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}\right)^{\frac{n}{2p^{\prime}}}(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2p}}
.

\square

Combining Proposition
3.1.7
and
3.2.1
we now establish the following remarkable instance of the
subordination principle
of Bochner.

Proposition 3.2.2

Let
n\geq 3
. Then the following equation holds in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\Delta_{y}\int_{0}^{\infty}G(x-y,t)\,dt=-\delta_{x},

where
\delta_{x}
indicates the Dirac delta at
x
.

Proof:

To establish the proposition we need to show that:

1.

for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the function
y\to\int_{0}^{\infty}G(x-y,t)\,dt
defines an element of
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
;

2.

for every
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
one has

\braket{\int_{0}^{\infty}G(x-y,t)\,dt,\Delta_{y}f}=-f(x).

Concerning (1), we notice the following remarkable fact

\int_{0}^{\infty}G(x-y,t)\,dt=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-2}{2}\right)}{4\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}\frac{1}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{n-2}}.

(3.9)

Equation (
3.9
) follows simply by the change of variable
\tau=\frac{\lvert x-y\rvert^{2}}{4t}
in the integral

\int_{0}^{\infty}G(x-y,t)\,dt=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{n-2}{2}}}e^{-\frac{\lvert x-y\rvert^{2}}{4t}}\frac{dt}{t}.

Since the function
y\to\lvert x-y\rvert^{2-n}
belongs to
L_{\text{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, conclusion (1) follows from (
3.9
). To prove (2) let
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We have by Fubini’s theorem

\begin{split}&\braket{\Delta_{y}\int_{0}^{\infty}G(x-y,t)\,dt,f}=\braket{\int_{0}^{\infty}G(x-y,t)\,dt,\Delta_{y}f}\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}G(x-y,t)\,dt\Delta_{y}f(y)\,dy=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-y,t)\Delta_{y}f(y)\,dy\,dt\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}P_{t}(\Delta f)(x)\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\Delta P_{t}f(x)\,dt,\end{split}

where in the last equality we have used (
3.8
). Since Proposition
3.1.6
gives
\Delta P_{t}f(x)=\partial_{t}P_{t}f(x)
, by the above computation we find

\begin{split}&\braket{\Delta_{y}\int_{0}^{\infty}G(x-y,t)\,dt,f}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{t}P_{t}f(x)\,dt=\lim_{T\to\infty,\,\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\epsilon}^{T}\partial_{t}P_{t}f(x)\,dt\\
&=\lim_{T\to\infty,\,\epsilon\to 0^{+}}[P_{T}f(x)-P_{\epsilon}f(x)]=-f(x),\end{split}

where in the last equality we have used (
3.5
) and (
3.20
).

\square

3.3
Fractional powers of the Laplacian

In the Chapter
2
we introduced the fractional Laplacian, see (
2.7
). In this section, we will not follow the original presentation of the subject, but instead use the semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and only subsequently recognise the equivalence of the two notions. The next definition was originally set forth by Balakrishnan and can be be deduced from his seminal works
2
,
3
.

Definition 3.3.1
(Balakrishnan)

Let
0<\alpha<2
. The fractional Laplacian of order
\frac{\alpha}{2}
is defined on a function
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by the formula

(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x)=-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt.

Our first observation is that Definition
3.3.1
makes sense, i.e., that the integral in the right-hand side is finite. This will be a consequence of the following result.

Lemma 3.3.2

There exists a constant
C(n)>0
such that for every
f\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with second derivatives in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have

\lvert P_{t}f(x)-f(x)\rvert\leq C(n)\lVert\nabla^{2}f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}t.

Proof:

As a first observation we claim that

P_{t}f(x)-f(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(y,t)[f(x+y)+f(x-y)-2f(x)]\,dy.

(3.10)

Next, we observe that the
C^{2}
Taylor formula gives

\begin{split}&f(x+y)=f(x)+\braket{\nabla f(x),y}+\frac{1}{2}\braket{\nabla^{2}f(y^{\star})y,y},\\
&f(x-y)=f(x)-\braket{\nabla f(x),y}+\frac{1}{2}\braket{\nabla^{2}f(y^{\star\star})y,y}.\end{split}

This implies

\lvert f(x+y)+f(x-y)-2f(x)\rvert\leq\lVert\nabla^{2}f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lvert y\rvert^{2}.

Substituting this estimate in (
3.10
) we obtain

\lvert P_{t}f(x)-f(x)\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nabla^{2}f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(y,t)\lvert y\rvert^{2}\,dy\leq C(n)\lVert\nabla^{2}f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}t,

This proves the desired result.

\square

With Lemma
3.3.2
we can now show that the integral defining
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x)
is finite for every
x\in Rn
. We have in fact

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}&\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt+\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt.\end{split}

The integral on
(0,1)
is finite thanks to Lemma
3.3.2
. The integral on
(1,\infty)
is trivially estimated as follows

\left\lvert\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt\right\rvert\leq 2\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\,dt<\infty,

where we have used (
3.3
) to infer
\lVert P_{t}f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
.

We close this section by establishing an ”integration by parts” formula for the operator
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
.

Proposition 3.3.3

Let
0<\alpha<2
. For any
f,g\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
one has

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[g(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f-f(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}g\right]\,dx=0.

Proof:

We have

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}&g(x)(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x)\,dx=-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}g(x)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt\,dx\\
&=-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}g(x)(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dx\,dt\\
&=-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(x)(P_{t}g(x)-g(x))\,dx\,dt\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(x)(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}g(x)\,dx.\end{split}

\square

We note that Proposition
3.3.3
continues to be true if we replace the hypothesis
g\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with bounded second derivatives. With this observation we obtain the following.

Corollary 3.3.4

For any
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
one has

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f\,dx=0.

3.4
Balakrishnan met M. Riesz

In this section we show that Balakrishnan’s definition of the nonlocal operator
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
coincides with that introduced by M. Riesz in
18
. Subsequently, we analyse the asymptotic behaviour of this operator as
\alpha\nearrow 2
and we show that, unsurprisingly, in the limit we obtain the negative of the Laplace operator
\Delta
.

Proposition 3.4.1

Let
0<\alpha<2
. For every
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
one has

(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x)=\frac{\alpha 2^{\alpha-2}\Gamma\left(\frac{n+\alpha}{2}\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2f(x)-f(x+y)-f(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+\alpha}}\,dy.

Proof:

Using equation (
3.10
) and Fubini’s theorem we find

\begin{split}(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}&f(x)=-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{2\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(y,t)[f(x+y)+f(x-y)-2f(x)]\,dy\,dt\\
&=\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{2\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}[f(x+y)+f(x-y)-2f(x)]\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}G(y,t)\,dt\,dy.\end{split}

To complete the proof all is needed at this point is the following elementary computation

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}&\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}G(y,t)\,dt=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{n+\alpha}{2}}}e^{-\frac{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}{4t}}\,\frac{dt}{t}\\
&=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}2^{n+\alpha}\Gamma\left(\frac{n+\alpha}{2}\right)\lvert y\rvert^{-(n+\alpha)}.\end{split}

(3.11)

\square

We next analyse the limit of
(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
as
\alpha\nearrow 2
.

Proposition 3.4.2

Let
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
one has

\lim_{\alpha\nearrow 2}(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x)=-\Delta f(x).

Proof:

We write

\begin{split}(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x)&=-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt\\
&=-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt.\\
&=\mathbb{I}(\alpha)+\mathbb{II}(\alpha).\end{split}

The second term is easily estimated as follows

\lvert\mathbb{II}(\alpha)\rvert\leq 2\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\,dt\to 0,\hskip 8.50012pt\text{as }\alpha\to 2,

since
\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)=1+o(1)
as
\alpha\nearrow 2
. For the first term we argue in the following way. By Lemma
3.3.2
we can integrate by parts obtaining

\begin{split}\mathbb{I}(\alpha)&=-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{t^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}}{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)^{\prime}(P_{t}f(x)-f(x))\,dt\\
&=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}(P_{1}f(x)-f(x))-\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\frac{d}{dt}P_{t}f(x)\,dt\\
&=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}(P_{1}f(x)-f(x))-\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\Delta P_{t}f(x)\,dt\\
&=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}(P_{1}f(x)-f(x))-\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}P_{t}\Delta f(x)\,dt.\end{split}

where in the last two equalities we have used Proposition
3.1.6
ans
3.1.7
. Since
\Delta f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, by Lemma
3.3.2
again we can write for
t\in[0,1]

P_{t}\Delta f(x)=\Delta f(x)+O(t).

Therefore

\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}P_{t}\Delta f(x)\,dt=\frac{1}{\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\Delta f(x)+o(1)

as
\alpha\nearrow 2
. Substituting in the above expression of
\mathbb{I}(\alpha)
we conclude that

\mathbb{I}(\alpha)\to-\Delta f(x),\hskip 17.00024pt\text{as }\alpha\nearrow 2,

thus completing the proof.

\square

3.5
The evolutive semigroup

In semigroup theory a procedure for forming a new semigroup from a given one is that of evolution semigroup. In this section we exploit this idea to introduce a new semigroup that will be used as a building block for: (i) defining the fractional powers of the heat operator
H=\Delta-\partial_{t}
; (ii) solve the extension problem for such nonlocal operators.

Let us to introduce the following operator on functions

P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-y,\tau)f(y,t-\tau)\,dy=P_{\tau}(\Lambda_{-\tau}f(\cdot,t))(x),

(3.12)

and call it the
evolutive semigroup
. The motivation for such name is in the fact that
\{P_{\tau}^{H}\}_{\tau>0}
does in fact define a semigroup of contractions on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, for
1\leq p\leq\infty
, where hereafter we use the notation
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
to indicate the space
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
with respect the variables
(x,t)
. One has in fact from
(
3.12
)
for
\tau,\sigma>0

\begin{split}P_{\tau+\sigma}^{H}f(x,t)&=P_{\tau+\sigma}(\Lambda_{-\tau-\sigma}f(\cdot,t))(x)\\
&=P_{\tau}(P_{\sigma}(\Lambda_{-\tau-\sigma}f(\cdot,t)))(x)\\
&=P_{\tau}(\Lambda_{-\tau}(P_{\sigma}(\Lambda_{-\sigma}f(\cdot,t))))(x)\\
&=P_{\tau}^{H}(P_{\sigma}^{H}f)(x,t).\end{split}

(3.13)

Furthermore, one has

\lim_{\tau\to 0^{+}}P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)=f(x,t).

(3.14)

The following two lemmas summarise the semigroup
P_{\tau}^{H}
properties that have already been proved for
P_{t}
.

Lemma 3.5.1

For any
t>0
we have:

1.

H(\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}))\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
and
P_{\tau}^{H}(\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}))\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
;

2.

For any
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
and
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
one has
\partial_{\tau}P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)=HP_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)
;

3.

For every
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
and
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
the commutation property is true

HP_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)=P_{\tau}^{H}Hf(x,t).

Proof:

(1) The first part is obvious. For the second part it suffices to show that
\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-9.33466pt\hbox{$P_{\tau}^{H}f$}\hskip-9.33466pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{9.24998pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoic 375.66913pt}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
if
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, and this follows from the following formula,

\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-9.33466pt\hbox{$P_{\tau}^{H}f$}\hskip-9.33466pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{9.24998pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoic 375.66913pt}(\xi,\sigma)=e^{-\tau(4\pi^{2}\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}+2\pi i\sigma)}\hbox{\hskip
