375.66913pt}(\xi,\sigma).

(3.15)

(2) Is a consequence of the definition of
P_{\tau}^{H}

(3) Follows immediately from (
3.15
) and the fact that

\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-6.282pt\hbox{$Hf$}\hskip-6.282pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{8.15555pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi,\sigma)=-(4\pi^{2}\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}+2\pi i\sigma)\hbox{\hskip 375.66913pt\hskip-2.48265pt\hbox{$f$}\hskip-2.48265pt\hskip-375.66913pt\raisebox{8.15555pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-0.49205pt}{\resizebox{44535609}{112867}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14221pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.59999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14224pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.12pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.784pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14114pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.79999pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.56pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-375.66913pt\hskip 375.66913pt}(\xi,\sigma),

or from the commutation property
\Delta P_{t}=P_{t}\Delta
in Proposition
3.1.7
, and from the relations
P_{\tau}^{H}f=P_{\tau}(\Lambda_{-\tau}f)
,
H\Lambda_{-\tau}=\Lambda_{-\tau}H
.

\square

Henceforth, we will often use some mixed Lebesgue spaces which represent the appropriate substitute for the standard
L^{p}
spaces when dealing with anisotropic partial differential operators such as the heat operator. Given a measurable function
f(x,t)
on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, and exponents
1\leq p
,
q\leq\infty
, we will write
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R},L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
to indicate the fact that

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R},L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert f(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty,

with obvious changes when
p=\infty
. It is clear that
L^{p}(\mathbb{R},L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
.

Lemma 3.5.2

The following properties hold:

1.

For every
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
\tau>0
we have
P_{\tau}^{H}1(x,t)=1
;

2.

We have
P_{\tau+\sigma}^{H}=P_{\tau}^{H}\circ P_{\sigma}^{H}
for every
\sigma,\tau>0
.

3.

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
, then
P_{\tau}^{H}:L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
with
\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}\rVert_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}\leq 1
. Therefore,
\{P_{\tau}^{H}\}_{\tau>0}
is a semigroup of contractions on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
when
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

Proof:

The proof (1) and (2) have already been given. We only provide the details of (3). If
p=\infty
, then it is immediate to see

\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})},

(3.16)

thus we assume that
1\leq p<\infty
. Using the second equality in (
3.12
), (
3.3
) and Tonelli’s theorem we have for any
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})

\begin{split}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}&=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert P_{\tau}(\Lambda_{-\tau}f(\cdot,t))\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert\Lambda_{-\tau}f(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert f(\cdot,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}=\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}.\end{split}

\square

We conclude this section with the analogue of Lemma
3.1.8
for the semigroup
\{P_{\tau}^{H}\}_{\tau>0}
and an important consequence of it.

Lemma 3.5.3

For every
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
and
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
we have

\lvert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rvert\leq\lVert Hf\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\tau.

Proof:

We have

P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)=\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{d}{d\sigma}P_{\sigma}^{H}f(x,t)\,d\sigma=\int_{0}^{\tau}HP_{\sigma}^{H}f(x,t)\,d\sigma=\int_{0}^{\tau}P_{\sigma}^{H}Hf(x,t)\,d\sigma,

where in the last equality we have used the commutation property (3) in Lemma
3.5.1
. This gives, using (
3.16
),

\begin{split}\lvert P_{\tau}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rvert&\leq\int_{0}^{\tau}\lvert P_{\sigma}^{H}Hf(x,t)\rvert\,d\sigma\leq\int_{0}^{\tau}\lVert P_{\sigma}^{H}Hf(x,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\sigma\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{\tau}\lVert Hf(x,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\sigma=\lVert Hf(x,t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\tau.\end{split}

\square

Arguing in a similar way one proves the following.

Lemma 3.5.4

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
. Given any
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
for any
\tau\in[0,1]
we have

\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq\lVert Hf\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\tau.

Corollary 3.5.5

Let
1\leq p<\infty
. For any
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
one has

\lim_{\tau\to 0^{+}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}=0.

As a consequence,
\{P_{\tau}^{H}\}_{\tau>0}
is a strongly continuous semigroup of contractions on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
.

Proof:

Since
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
is dense in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
such that

\lVert f-\psi\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}<\frac{\epsilon}{3}.

Having fixed
\psi
in this way, in view of Lemma
3.5.4
there exists
\tau_{0}=\tau_{0}(\epsilon)>0
such that for every
0<\tau<\tau_{0}
we have

\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}\psi-\psi\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}<\frac{\epsilon}{3}.

This gives for every
0<\tau<\tau_{0}

\begin{split}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}&\leq\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}(f-\psi)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}+\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}\psi-\psi\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}+\lVert\psi-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\\
&\leq\lVert\psi-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}<\epsilon,\end{split}

where we have used (3) of Lemma
3.5.2
.

\square

3.6
The fractional heat operator

With the results of Section
3.5
in hand we are now ready to introduce the fractional powers
(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
.

Definition 3.6.1

Let
0<\alpha<2
. The fractional heat operator of order
\frac{\alpha}{2}
is defined on a function
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
by the formula

(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x,t)=-\frac{\frac{\alpha}{2}}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}(P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t))\,d\tau.

We observe right-away that, thanks to (
3.16
) and Lemma
3.5.3
the integral in the right-hand side of (
3.6.1
) is finite. It is also worth observing that
f(x,t)=f(x)
, then

(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x,t)=(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x).

Next, we observe that if we presently define the parabolic dilations

\delta_{\lambda}f(x,t)=f(\lambda x,\lambda^{2}t),

the simple manipulations show that

P_{\tau}^{H}(\delta_{\lambda}f)(x,t)=P_{\lambda^{2}\tau}^{H}f(\lambda x,\lambda^{2}t).

(3.17)

One easily obtains from (
3.17
)

(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(\delta_{\lambda}f)(x,t)=\lambda^{\alpha}(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(\lambda x,\lambda^{2}t),

(3.18)

which shows that the fractional heat is an operator of order
\alpha
with respect to the anisotropic parabolic dilations.

We conclude this section with the next result, which is an useful version of Proposition
3.2.1

Proposition 3.6.2
(weak Ultracontractivity)

Let
1\leq q<\infty
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. For every
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
\tau>0
we have

\lvert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)\rvert\leq\frac{c(n,q)}{\tau^{\frac{n}{2q}}}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))},

(3.19)

for a certain constant
c(n,q)>0
. In particular, for any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
one has

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\lvert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)\rvert=0.

(3.20)

Proof:

Applying Hölder’s inequality to (
3.12
) we find

\begin{split}\lvert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)\rvert&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-y,\tau)\lvert f(y,t-\tau)\rvert\,dy\\
&\leq\lVert f(\cdot,t-\tau)\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-y,\tau)^{q^{\prime}}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}}\\
&\leq c(n,q)\tau^{-\frac{n}{2q}}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))},\end{split}

where
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
.

\square

3.7
The extension problem

In their seminal 2007 paper
7
Caffarelli and Silvestre introduced the
extension problem
for the fractional powers of the Laplacian, that we have already proved in section
2.5
. In line with the spirit of the present chapter we are going to use the heat semigroup to solve, again, the
extension problem
.

In what follows we consider the upper half-space
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}_{y}^{+}
, with variable
X=(x,y)
, where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
y>0
. Let
0<\alpha<2
and introduce what we call the
Bessel parameter

a=1-\alpha\in(-1,1)
. Given a function
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
we want to find a function
U\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
such that

\begin{cases}y^{a}\partial_{t}U-\text{div}_{X}(y^{a}\nabla_{X}U)=0\hskip 8.50012pt\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{+},\\
U(x,t,0)=f(x,t)\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 8.50012pt(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.\end{cases}

(3.21)

When
\alpha=1
, and therefore
a=0
, the problem (
3.21
) was first introduced and solved by Frank Jones in his beautiful but apparently not so well-known 1968 paper
15
. He also proved the following identity

-\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}\frac{\partial U}{\partial y}(x,t,y)=(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}f(x,t).

(3.22)

More recently, Nyström & Sande
17
and Stinga & Torrea
22
have independently generalized Jones’ results to all fractional powers
0<s<1
. Let us notice right-away that since the right-hand side of the PDE in (
3.21
) is zero, we can factor
y^{a}
out and write the problem in the equivalent form

\begin{cases}\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}U=\partial_{yy}U+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}U+\Delta_{x}U-\partial_{t}U=0\hskip 8.50012pt\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{+},\\
U(x,t,0)=f(x,t)\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 8.50012pt(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.\end{cases}

(3.23)

We call
\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}
the
extension operator
. To find its heat kernel we make the following formal considerations, which can be justified a posteriori. Denote by
w
a point in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{a+1}
with fractional dimension
a+1
. Never mind for the time being the fact that this really makes no sense. If we denote by
y=\lvert w\rvert
(again, this is purely formal), then the Laplacian in the variable
w
, restricted to functions having spherical symmetry, takes the form

\Delta_{w}=\partial_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}.

This suggests that we should look at the following PDE in
\mathbb{R}^{n+a+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}

\Delta_{w}U+\Delta_{x}U-\partial_{t}U=0.

(3.24)

The heat kernel for (
3.24
) is given by by the Gaussian in
\mathbb{R}^{n+a+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}

G^{(a)}((x,w),t)=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n+a+1}{2}}e^{-\lvert(x,w)\rvert^{2}}4t=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n+a+1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}+y^{2}}{4t}},

(3.25)

where we have used the ”assumption” that
y=\lvert w\rvert
. Notice that the function (
3.25
) is nothing but the product of the heat operator in a product space such as (
3.24
). Since the
Bessel operator
on the half-line
\mathbb{R}_{y}^{+}

\mathscr{B}^{(a)}=\partial_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}

is self-adjoint with respect to the measure
y^{a}\,dy
, and since from (
3.25
) we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}_{y}^{+}}G^{(a)}((x,w),t)y^{a}\,dy\,dx=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{a+1}{2}\right)}{2\pi^{\frac{a+1}{2}}},

we normalise
G^{(a)}((x,w),t)
as follows

\mathscr{G}^{(a)}(x-z,y,t)=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{a+1}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{a+1}{2}\right)}(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n+a+1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\lvert x-z\rvert^{2}+y^{2}}{4t}}.

(3.26)

In this way we have for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t>0

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}_{y}^{+}}\mathscr{G}^{(a)}(x-z,y,t)y^{a}\,dy\,dz=1.

(3.27)

For reasons that will soon be clear, along with the partial differential operator
\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}
in (
3.23
) we ought to also consider its
intertwined
operator in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}

\mathfrak{B}^{(2-a)}U=\partial_{yy}U+\frac{2-a}{y}\partial_{y}U+\Delta_{x}U-\partial_{t}U,

(3.28)

whose heat kernel in
\mathbb{R}^{n+3-a}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
is given by

G^{(2-a)}((x,w),t)=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n+3-a}{2}}e^{-\lvert(x,w)\rvert^{2}}4t=(4\pi t)^{-\frac{n+3-a}{2}}e^{-\frac{\lvert x\rvert^{2}+y^{2}}{4t}}.

(3.29)

The motivation for introducing
(
3.28
)
is in the following intertwining equation for the Bessel operators

\mathscr{B}^{(a)}(y^{1-a}U)=y^{1-a}\mathscr{B}^{(2-a)}U,

(3.30)

that the reader can easily verify. The equation (
3.30
) shows that
U
solves
\mathscr{B}^{(2-a)}U=0
if and only if
\mathscr{B}^{(a)}(y^{1-a}U)=0
. As a consequence, we have the corresponding intertwining relation

\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}(y^{1-a}U)=y^{1-a}\mathfrak{B}^{(2-a)}U.

(3.31)

This lead us to introduce the following.

Definition 3.7.1

We define the Poisson kernel of the operator
\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}
as the function

\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,t)=\frac{1}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}\frac{y^{1-a}}{t^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4t}}G(x-z,t).

We note right-away that, since up to a constant function

(x,y,t)\to\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}G(x-z,t)

is for every fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the heat kernel (
3.29
), it is in particular a solution of the equation
\mathfrak{B}^{(2-a)}U=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}_{y}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}^{+}
. In view of (
3.31
) we deduce that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the function

(z,y,t)\to\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,t)

is a solution of the equation

\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,t)=0

(3.32)

in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}_{y}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}^{+}
. Furthermore, we have the following.

Proposition 3.7.2

For every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
y>0
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,t)\,dz\,dt=1.

Proof:

By Definition
3.7.1
and the theorem of Tonelli we have

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}&\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,t)\,dz\,dt=\frac{y^{1-a}}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4t}}G(x-z,t)\,dz\,dt\\
&=\frac{y^{1-a}}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-z,t)\,dz\right)\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4t}}\,dt\\
&=\frac{y^{1-a}}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4t}}\,dt=1,\end{split}

where the reader can easily verify the last equality by the change of variable
\sigma=\frac{y^{2}}{4t}
.

\square

With Definition
3.7.1
in hands we can now solve problem (
3.23
). The following is the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.7.3

Given
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, consider the function defined by the equation

U(x,y,t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau.

(3.33)

Then,
U\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times(0,\infty))
, and for any
1\leq p\leq\infty
the function
U
solves the extension problem in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, in sense that we have
\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}U=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times(0,\infty)
, and moreover

\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}\lVert U(\cdot,y,\cdot)-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}=0.

(3.34)

Furthermore, we also have in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})

-\frac{2^{-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1+a}{2}\right)}\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}y^{a}\partial_{y}U(\cdot,y,\cdot)=(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f.

(3.35)

Proof:

Using the Gaussian character of the function
\mathscr{P}^{(a)}
in Definition
3.7.1
, it is not difficult to justify differentiating under the integral sign in (
3.33
). By iteration one thus proves that
U\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times(0,\infty))
. Furthermore, since by (
3.33
)

\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}U(x,y,t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau

in view of (
3.32
) we see that

\mathfrak{B}^{(a)}U(x,y,t)=0,

in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times(0,\infty)
. Perhaps it is worth noting here that

\begin{split}\partial_{t}U(x,y,t)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,\tau)\partial_{t}f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,\tau)\partial_{\tau}f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{\tau}\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau,\end{split}

where in the last equality we have integrated by parts. We are thus left with proving (
3.34
) and (
3.35
). To reach this goal we make the crucial observation that
U
can be written in the following form using the semigroup
P_{\tau}^{H}

U(x,y,t)=\frac{1}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}y^{1-a}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)\,d\tau.

(3.36)

To recognise the validity of (
3.36
) we use (
3.33
) and Definition
3.7.1
to find

\begin{split}U(x,y,t)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(a)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=\frac{1}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}y^{1-a}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-z,t)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\right)\,d\tau\end{split}

=\frac{1}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}y^{1-a}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)\,d\tau,

which proves (
3.36
). In view of Proposition
3.7.2
we obtain from (
3.36
)

U(x,y,t)-f(x,t)=\frac{1}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}y^{1-a}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}[P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)]\,d\tau.

(3.37)

Using the representation (
3.37
) we thus find

\begin{split}&\lVert U(\cdot,y,\cdot)-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}y^{1-a}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\\
&+\frac{1}{2^{1-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}y^{1-a}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau.\end{split}

In the second integral we use the contractivity of
P_{\tau}^{H}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
to bound

\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq 2\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}\in L^{1}(1,\infty),

since
\frac{3-a}{2}>1
. In the first integral, instead, we need to crucially use the rate in Lemma
3.5.4

\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}=O(\tau),

to estimate

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\leq C\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{1-a}{2}}}\,d\tau<\infty,

since
0<\frac{1-a}{2}<1
. In conclusion, the right-hand side in (
3.37
) goes to
0
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
norm with
y^{1-a}
, and since
1-a>0
, we have proved (
3.34
).

In order to complete the proof we are left with establishing (
3.35
). Differentiating with respect to
y
the representation formula (
3.37
), we find

\begin{split}-\frac{2^{-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1+a}{2}\right)}&y^{a}\partial_{y}U(x,y,t)\\
&=-\frac{1-a}{2\Gamma\left(\frac{1+a}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}[P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)]\,d\tau\\
&+\frac{1}{4\Gamma\left(\frac{1+a}{2}\right)}y^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}[P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)]\frac{d\tau}{\tau}.\end{split}

(3.38)

On the other hand, keeping the equation
a=1-\alpha
in mind, we can express

(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(x,t)=-\frac{1-a}{2\Gamma\left(\frac{1+a}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}[P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)]\,d\tau.

(3.39)

Subtracting (
3.39
) from (
3.38
) we thus find

\begin{split}&\left\lVert-\frac{2^{-a}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-a}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1+a}{2}\right)}y^{a}\partial_{y}U(\cdot,y,\cdot)-(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\\
&\leq\frac{1-a}{2\Gamma\left(\frac{1+a}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}\left\lvert e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}-1\right\rvert\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\\
&+\frac{1}{4\Gamma\left(\frac{1+a}{2}\right)}y^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\frac{d\tau}{\tau}\\
&=\mathbb{I}(y)+\mathbb{II}(y).\end{split}

To complete the proof of the theorem it suffices to show that both
\mathbb{I}(y),\mathbb{II}(y)\to 0
as
y\to 0^{+}
. We handle
\mathbb{II}(y)
as follows

\begin{split}\mathbb{II}(y)&\equiv y^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{1-a}{2}}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\frac{d\tau}{\tau}+y^{2}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}\frac{d\tau}{\tau}\\
&=O(y^{1+a})\to 0\hskip 8.50012pt\text{since }a\in(-1,1).\end{split}

For
\mathbb{I}(y)
we consider the integrand

0\leq g_{y}(\tau):=\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}\left\lvert e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}-1\right\rvert\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})},\hskip 17.00024pt0<\tau<\infty.

We clearly have
g_{y}(\tau)\to 0
as
y\to 0^{+}
for every
\tau>0
. On the other hand, there exist an absolute constant
C>0
and a function
g\in L^{1}(0,\infty)
such that
0\leq g_{y}(\tau)\leq Cg(\tau)
for every
\tau>0
. In fact, using Lemmas
3.5.2
and
3.5.4
it is not difficult to convince oneself that we can take

g(\tau)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{1-a}{2}}}\hskip 17.00024pt0<\tau<1,\\
\frac{1}{\tau^{\frac{3-a}{2}}}\hskip 17.00024pt1<\tau<\infty.\end{cases}

By Lebesgue dominated convergence we conclude that
\mathbb{I}(y)\to 0
as
y\to 0^{+}
.

\square

Chapter 4
Higher order

4.1
Fractional operators of higher order

In this section we want to introduce the fractional operators of higher order considered in the previous chapters, that is, the fractional Laplacean
(-\Delta)^{s}
, see also
1
, and the fractional heat operator
(-H)^{s}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
. In Chapter
2
we introduced in Definition
2.1.4
the fractional Laplacean with
s\in(0,1)
,

(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=\frac{\gamma(n,s)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{2u(x)-u(x+y)-u(x-y)}{\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}}\,dy.

The limitation
s<1
is due to the singularity of the kernel
\lvert y\rvert^{n+2s}
and, therefore, the same formula does not carry over to
s>1
.

To introduce the Laplacean of higher order we start from its formulation according to Balakrishnan in Definition
3.3.1
in which we take
s=\frac{\alpha}{2}
,

(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=-\frac{s}{\Gamma\left(1-s\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+s}}(P_{t}u(x)-u(x))\,dt,

(4.1)

which is also written as

(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=-\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-s\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{s}}(-\partial_{t})P_{t}u(x)\,dt.

(4.2)

An advantage of the formulations (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) is that they admit the following natural generalization to higher powers

Definition 4.1.1

For every
s>0
non-integer we write

s=k+\sigma,\hskip 17.00024pt\text{where }k=[s]:=\max\{d\in\mathbb{Z}:d<s\}\hskip 8.50012pt\text{ and }\hskip 8.50012pt\sigma\in[0,1)

and we define

\begin{split}(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)&=(-\Delta)^{\sigma}\left((-\Delta)^{k}u\right)(x)\\
&=-\frac{\sigma}{\Gamma\left(1-\sigma\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{1+\sigma}}(P_{t}(-\Delta)^{k}u(x)-(-\Delta)^{k}u(x))\,dt\\
&=-\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-\sigma\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{\sigma}}(-\partial_{t})^{k+1}P_{t}u(x)\,dt.\end{split}

(4.3)

This formula easily follows from one of the main properties of the heat semigroup; indeed, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have
(-\partial_{t})^{k}P_{t}u(x)=(-\Delta)^{k}(P_{t}u)(x)=P_{t}(-\Delta)^{k}u(x)
, and from the Definition
4.1.1
it follows that

(-\Delta)^{s}u(x)=(-\Delta)^{\sigma}\left((-\Delta)^{k}u\right)(x)

where
(-\Delta)^{\sigma}
is defined through (
4.2
).

Analogously, we can naturally define the fractional heat operator as

Definition 4.1.2

For every
s\geq 0
we write

s=k+\sigma,\hskip 17.00024pt\text{where }k=[s]:=\max\{d\in\mathbb{Z}:d<s\}\hskip 8.50012pt\text{ and }\hskip 8.50012pt\sigma\in[0,1)

and we define

\begin{split}(-H)^{s}u(x,t)&=(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{s}u(x,t)=(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\sigma}\left((\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{k}u\right)(x,t)=(-1)^{k}(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{\sigma}\left((H)^{k}u\right)(x,t)\\
&=-(-1)^{k}\frac{\sigma}{\Gamma\left(1-\sigma\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+\sigma}}(P_{\tau}^{H}H^{k}u(x,t)-H^{k}u(x,t))\,d\tau\\
\end{split}

(4.4)

4.2
Extension problem of higher order

The goal of this chapter is to generalize the Caffarelli-Silvestre extension problem to higher powers of the heat operator. In the time independent setting, Successful attempts in such direction appeared in
5
(using conformal geometry techniques), see also
23
,
8
and
6
.

In accordance with our work in Section
3.7
, and differently from the previously mentioned articles, we are going to focus on the heat counterpart of the extension problem (
2.85
).

In preparation for the main result of this section, we recall that we write
H=\Delta-\partial_{t}
for the heat operator, and define

\mathscr{H}_{a}:=\partial_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}+H=\partial_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}+\Delta-\partial_{t}.

Furthermore, the integer part of a real number
s
is expressed as follows

[s]:=\max\{d\in\mathbb{Z}:d<s\}.

In order to prove the Theorem
4.2.2
, we first need to formalize the Poisson kernel for the equation (
4.6
). Thereby, following the blueprint of section
3.7
, we define the following function:

Definition 4.2.1

for
s>0
non-integer

\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,t):=\frac{1}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}\frac{y^{2s}}{t^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4t}}G(x-z,t)

(4.5)

The following is the main result of this section.

Theorem 4.2.2

Let
s>0
be some non-integer and
a=1-2(s-[s])
. Given
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, consider the function defined by the equation

U(x,y,t):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau.

Then the function
U
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times(0,\infty)
solves

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]+1}U(x,y,t)=\left(\partial_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}+H\right)^{[s]+1}U(x,y,t)=0,

(4.6)

and for any
1\leq p\leq\infty
we have

\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}\lVert U(\cdot,y,\cdot)-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}=0.

(4.7)

Moreover, for every odd integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k\leq[s]
, we have

\lim_{y\to 0}y^{a}\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial y^{k}}U(x,y,t)=0.

(4.8)

Furthermore, we also have in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})

(-H)^{\,s}f(x,t)=(\partial_{t}-\Delta)^{s}f(x,t)=K(s)\lim_{y\to 0}y^{a}\partial_{y}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}U(x,y,t),

(4.9)

where

K(s):=-(-1)^{[s]}\frac{\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma(1+[s]-s)}\frac{2^{2(s-[s])-1}}{[s]!}.

4.2.1
Proof of the Theorem
4.2.2

We want to prove that
\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]+1}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=0
. Let us start with the computation of
\mathscr{H}_{(a)}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}
, and therefore:

\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\frac{2s(1-2(s-[s]))}{y^{2}}\left(\mathscr{P}^{(s)}-\mathscr{P}^{(s+1)}\right),

(4.10)

\partial_{yy}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\frac{4s^{2}-2s}{y^{2}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}-\frac{8s^{2}+2s}{y^{2}}\mathscr{P}^{(s+1)}+\frac{4s^{2}+4s}{y^{2}}\mathscr{P}^{(s+2)}.

(4.11)

Summing the (
4.10
) with (
4.11
) we have

\left(\partial_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}\right)\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\frac{4s[s]}{y^{2}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}-\frac{4s(1+s+[s])}{y^{2}}\mathscr{P}^{(s+1)}+\frac{4s(s+1)}{y^{2}}\mathscr{P}^{(s+2)}.

Now we compute the heat operator of
\mathscr{P}^{(s)}
:

\begin{split}H\mathscr{P}^{(s)}&=\frac{4s(1+s)}{y^{2}}\left(\mathscr{P}^{(s+1)}-\mathscr{P}^{(s+2)}\right).\end{split}

(4.12)

Thus, we easily obtain:

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\left(\partial_{yy}+\frac{a}{y}\partial_{y}+H\right)\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\frac{4s[s]}{y^{2}}\left(\mathscr{P}^{(s)}-\mathscr{P}^{(s+1)}\right).

Trivially then

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s])}=\frac{4(s-[s])[s-[s]]}{y^{2}}\left(\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s])}-\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s]+1)}\right)=0.

(4.13)

Our aim is then to compute
\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}
, starting from the observation that:

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\frac{[s]}{s-1}H\mathscr{P}^{(s-1)},

(4.14)

and iterating

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{2}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\frac{[s]([s]-1)}{(s-1)(s-2)}H^{2}\mathscr{P}^{(s-2)},

\begin{split}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{3}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}&=\frac{[s]([s]-1)([s]-2)}{(s-1)(s-2)(s-3)}H^{3}\mathscr{P}^{(s-3)}.\end{split}

Thereby, for
k\in\{1,2,\dots,[s]\}
, we have:

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{k}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\frac{[s]!}{([s]-k)!}\frac{\Gamma(s-k)}{\Gamma(s)}H^{k}\mathscr{P}^{(s-k)}.

Consequently, for
k=[s]
we get:

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=[s]!\frac{\Gamma(s-[s])}{\Gamma(s)}H^{[s]}\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s])}.

(4.15)

Finally we can directly show that
\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]+1}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=0
. Starting from (
4.15
) and exploiting (
4.13
) we have:

\begin{split}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]+1}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}&=\mathscr{H}_{(a)}\left([s]!\frac{\Gamma(s-[s])}{\Gamma(s)}H^{[s]}\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s])}\right)\\
&=[s]!\frac{\Gamma(s-[s])}{\Gamma(s)}H^{[s]}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s])}\\
&=0.\end{split}

Thus we have shown that

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]+1}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=0.

(4.16)

By virtue of the previous passages, we are ready to prove that:

U(x,y,t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau

is solution of

\begin{cases}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]+1}U=0&\text{in $\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}$}\\
U(x,0,t)=f(x,t)&\text{per $(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$}.\end{cases}

Indeed, we have the following relation:

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}U(x,y,t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau,

which follows from:

\begin{split}\partial_{t}U(x,y,t)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)\partial_{t}f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)\partial_{\tau}f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{\tau}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau,\end{split}

and consequently, hinging on (
4.16
) we easily obtain:

\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]+1}U(x,y,t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]+1}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau=0.

Now we show the following Lemma:

Lemma 4.2.3

For every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
y>0
and
s>0
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,t)\,dz\,dt=1.

Proof:

It can be directly shown through:

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,t)\,dz\,dt=\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}G(x-z,t)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-z,t)\,dz\right)\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\,d\tau\\
&=\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\,d\tau=1.\end{split}

\square

Now we want to reformulate the function
U
as:

\begin{split}U(x,y,t)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(x-z,t)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\right)\,d\tau\\
&=\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)\,d\tau.\end{split}

(4.17)

Thus, thanks to Lemma (
4.2.3
), we get:

U(x,y,t)-f(x,t)=\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left(P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)-f(x,t)\right)\,d\tau.

(4.18)

Through the use of (
4.18
) we manage to prove (
4.7
); indeed

\begin{split}\lVert U(\cdot,y,\cdot)&-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq\\
&\leq\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\\
&+\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau.\end{split}

For the second integral we exploit the contractivity of
P_{\tau}^{H}

\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq\frac{2}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\in L^{1}(1,\infty).

For the first integral we avail ourselves of the fact that
\tau\in(0,1)

\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}=O(\tau).

(4.19)

Hence, for the first integral we have

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\leq C\int_{0}^{1}\frac{e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}}{\tau^{s}}\,d\tau,

where the last integral with the change of variables
\omega=\frac{1}{\tau}
we obtain

C\int_{0}^{1}\frac{e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}}{\tau^{s}}\,d\tau=C\int_{1}^{\infty}\omega^{s-2}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4}\omega}\,d\omega.

Now we call on formula 3.381 of
13
, which claims that

\int_{u}^{\infty}x^{\nu-1}e^{-\mu x}\,dx=\mu^{-\nu}\int_{\mu u}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{\nu-1}\,dt\hskip 8.50012pt\text{per }u>0,\,\,\mathfrak{Re}\mu>0.

In our case, we have
\nu=s-1
,
u=1
and
\mu=\frac{y^{2}}{4}
, and therefore

C\int_{1}^{\infty}\omega^{s-2}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4}\omega}\,d\omega=C\frac{y^{2-2s}}{4^{1-s}}\int_{\frac{y^{2}}{4}}^{\infty}e^{-\omega}\omega^{s-1-1}\,d\omega\leq C\frac{y^{2-2s}}{4^{1-s}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\omega}\omega^{s-1-1}\,d\omega=C\frac{\Gamma(s-1)}{4^{1-s}}y^{2-2s}.

Thus, for the first integral we get

\begin{split}\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}&\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}f-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\leq\frac{y^{2s}}{2^{2s}\Gamma(s)}C\frac{\Gamma(s-1)}{4^{1-s}}y^{2-2s}\\
&=\frac{C}{4}\frac{\Gamma(s-1)}{\Gamma(s)}y^{2}\to 0\hskip 8.50012pt\text{as }y\to 0^{+},\end{split}

and we have shown that

\lim_{y\to 0}\lVert U(\cdot,y,\cdot)-f\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}=0.

Now we want to prove (
4.8
), starting from the observation that

\partial_{y}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\frac{y}{2(s-1)}H\mathscr{P}^{(s-1)},

(4.20)

and iterating

\partial_{y}^{3}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=3\frac{y}{2^{2}}\frac{1}{(s-1)(s-2)}H^{2}\mathscr{P}^{(s-2)}+\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)^{3}\frac{1}{(s-1)(s-2)(s-3)}H^{3}\mathscr{P}^{(s-3)},

\begin{split}\partial_{y}^{5}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}&=15\frac{y}{2^{3}}\frac{1}{(s-1)(s-2)(s-3)}H^{3}\mathscr{P}^{(s-3)}+10\frac{y^{3}}{2^{4}}\frac{1}{(s-1)(s-2)(s-3)(s-4)}H^{4}\mathscr{P}^{(s-4)}\\
&\hskip 17.00024pt+\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)^{5}\frac{1}{(s-1)(s-2)(s-3)(s-4)(s-5)}H^{5}\mathscr{P}^{(s-5)}.\end{split}

Thereby, we obtain the following iterative formula for suitable constants
c_{k}(i)>0
and with
k\in\{1,2,\dots,[s]\}
such that
k
is odd

\partial_{y}^{k}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}=\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}c_{k}(i)\frac{y^{k-2i}}{2^{k-i}}\frac{\Gamma(s-k)}{\Gamma(s)}H^{k-i}\mathscr{P}^{(s-k+i)}.

(4.21)

By means of (
4.21
) we can write

\begin{split}\partial_{y}^{k}U(x,y,t)&=\partial_{y}^{k}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\right)\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{y}^{k}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}c_{k}(i)\frac{y^{k-2i}}{2^{k-i}}\frac{\Gamma(s-k)}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}H^{k-i}\mathscr{P}^{(s-k+i)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}c_{k}(i)\frac{y^{k-2i}}{2^{k-i}}\frac{\Gamma(s-k)}{\Gamma(s)}H^{k-i}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s-k+i)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\right)\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}c_{k}(i)\frac{y^{k-2i}}{2^{k-i}}\frac{\Gamma(s-k)}{\Gamma(s)}H^{k-i}\left(\frac{y^{2(s-k+i)}}{2^{2(s-k+i)}\Gamma(s-k+i)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-k+i}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)\,d\tau\right)\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}c_{k}(i)\frac{y^{k-2i}}{2^{k-i}}\frac{\Gamma(s-k)}{\Gamma(s)}\frac{y^{2(s-k+i)}}{2^{2(s-k+i)}\Gamma(s-k+i)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-k+i}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}H^{k-i}f(x,t)\,d\tau.\end{split}

Then for

A_{k}(i,s)=\frac{c_{k}(i)}{2^{3k-3i-2s}}\frac{\Gamma(s-k)}{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(s-k+i)}>0,

we have

\partial_{y}^{k}U(x,y,t)=\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}A_{k}(i,s)y^{2s-k}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-k+i}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}H^{k-i}f(x,t)\,d\tau,

and clearly

y^{a}\partial_{y}^{k}U(x,y,t)=\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}A_{k}(i,s)y^{1+2[s]-k}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-k+i}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}H^{k-i}f(x,t)\,d\tau.

Finally, we can compute

\begin{split}\left\lVert y^{a}\partial_{y}^{k}U(\cdot,y,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}&\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}A_{k}(i,s)y^{1+2[s]-k}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-k+i}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}H^{k-i}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\\
&\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}B_{k}(i,s)y^{1+2[s]-k}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-k+i}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\,d\tau\\
&\leq C_{k}(i,s)y^{1+2[s]+k-2s-2i}\to 0\hskip 8.50012pt\text{ as }y\to 0.\end{split}

As a matter of fact
k+1-2(i+s-[s])>0
for every
i\in\{0,1,\dots,\frac{k-1}{2}\}
.

Thus we have proved that (
4.8
) for
k
odd such that
k\leq[s]
.

Now we want to prove (
4.9
). Exploiting (
4.17
) we obtain

\begin{split}H^{[s]}&\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s])}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\right)\\
&\hskip 8.50012pt=H^{[s]}\left(\frac{y^{2(s-[s])}}{2^{2(s-[s])}\Gamma(s-[s])}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}f(x,t)\,d\tau\right)\\
&\hskip 8.50012pt=\frac{y^{2(s-[s])}}{2^{2(s-[s])}\Gamma(s-[s])}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)\,d\tau,\end{split}

and consequently

\begin{split}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}U(x,y,t)&=\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}\mathscr{P}^{(s)}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=[s]!\frac{\Gamma(s-[s])}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}H^{[s]}\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s])}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\\
&=[s]!\frac{\Gamma(s-[s])}{\Gamma(s)}H^{[s]}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s])}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\right)\\
&=[s]!\frac{\Gamma(s-[s])}{\Gamma(s)}\frac{y^{2(s-[s])}}{2^{2(s-[s])}\Gamma(s-[s])}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)\,d\tau\\
&=[s]!\frac{y^{2(s-[s])}}{2^{2(s-[s])}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)\,d\tau.\end{split}

(4.22)

Hence

\begin{split}&y^{a}\partial_{y}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{s}U(x,y,t)=\\
&\hskip 8.50012pt=y^{a}\partial_{y}\left([s]!\frac{\Gamma(s-[s])}{\Gamma(s)}H^{[s]}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathscr{P}^{(s-[s])}(x-z,y,\tau)f(z,t-\tau)\,dz\,d\tau\right)\right)\\
&\hskip 8.50012pt=y^{a}\partial_{y}\left([s]!\frac{y^{2(s-[s])}}{2^{2(s-[s])}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{s+1-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)\,d\tau\right)\\
&\hskip 8.50012pt=\frac{(s-[s])[s]!}{2^{2(s-[s])-1}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)\,d\tau\\
&\hskip 17.00024pt-\frac{[s]!y^{2}}{2^{2(s-[s])+1}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{2+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)\,d\tau.\end{split}

(4.23)

Now we observe that:

\begin{split}\partial_{y}\left(\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}U(x,y,t)\right)&=\partial_{y}\left(\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}U(x,y,t)-[s]!\frac{\Gamma(s-[s])}{\Gamma(s)}H^{[s]}f(x,t)\right)\\
&=\partial_{y}\left([s]!\frac{y^{2(s-[s])}}{2^{2(s-[s])}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left(P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)-H^{[s]}f(x,t)\right)\,d\tau\right),\end{split}

and therefore

\begin{split}y^{a}&\partial_{y}\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}U(x,y,t)=y^{a}\partial_{y}\left(\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}U(x,y,t)-[s]!\frac{\Gamma(s-[s])}{\Gamma(s)}H^{[s]}f(x,t)\right)\\
&=\frac{(s-[s])[s]!}{2^{2(s-[s])-1}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left(P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)-H^{[s]}f(x,t)\right)\,d\tau\\
&\hskip 17.00024pt-\frac{[s]!y^{2}}{2^{2(s-[s])+1}\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{2+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left(P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)-H^{[s]}f(x,t)\right)\,d\tau.\end{split}

(4.24)

Multiplying (
4.24
) for the following constant

K(s):=-(-1)^{[s]}\frac{\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma(1+[s]-s)}\frac{2^{2(s-[s])-1}}{[s]!},

(
4.24
) becomes:

\begin{split}K(s)&y^{a}\partial_{y}\left(\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}U(x,y,t)\right)\\
&=-(-1)^{[s]}\frac{s-[s]}{\Gamma(1+[s]-s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left(P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)-H^{[s]}f(x,t)\right)\,d\tau\\
&\hskip 17.00024pt+(-1)^{[s]}\frac{y^{2}}{4\Gamma(1+[s]-s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{2+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left(P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)-H^{[s]}f(x,t)\right)\,d\tau.\end{split}

(4.25)

From the Definition
4.1.2
of the fractional heat operator of higher order, for
k=[s]
and
\sigma=s-[s]
we have

\begin{split}(-H)^{s}f(x,t)&=(-1)^{[s]}(-H)^{s-[s]}(H^{[s]}f)(x,t)=\\
&=-(-1)^{[s]}\frac{s-[s]}{\Gamma\left(1-s+[s]\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+\sigma}}(P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f(x,t)-H^{[s]}f(x,t))\,d\tau,\end{split}

and finally we can compute:

\begin{split}&\left\lVert K(s)y^{a}\partial_{y}\left(\mathscr{H}_{(a)}^{[s]}U(\cdot,y,\cdot)\right)-(-H)^{s}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\leq\\
&\hskip 8.50012pt\leq\frac{s-[s]}{\Gamma(1-s+[s])}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}\left\lvert e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}-1\right\rvert\left\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f-H^{[s]}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\\
&\hskip 17.00024pt+\frac{y^{2}}{4\Gamma(1+[s]-s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{2+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f-H^{[s]}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\\
&\hskip 8.50012pt=\mathbb{I}(y)+\mathbb{II}(y).\end{split}

Thus we are left with showing that
\mathbb{I}(y),\mathbb{II}(y)\to 0
for
y\to 0
.

Indeed, for
\mathbb{II}(y)
we have:

\begin{split}\mathbb{II}(y)&\sim y^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{2+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f-H^{[s]}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\\
&\hskip 17.00024pt+y^{2}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau^{2+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f-H^{[s]}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau,\end{split}

where for the first integral we use the formula (
4.19
) to obtain the following estimate

y^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{2+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\left\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f-H^{[s]}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}\,d\tau\leq C_{2}y^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\,d\tau.

Now, after the change of variables
\omega=\frac{1}{\tau}
and using the formula 3.381 of
13
we obtain

C_{2}y^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}\,d\tau\leq\frac{C_{2}\Gamma(s-[s])}{4^{[s]-s}}y^{2([s]-s+1)}\to 0\hskip 8.50012pt\text{as }\,t\to 0,

since
[s]-s+1>0
. Instead, the second integral is finite for
2+s-[s]>1
and therefore

\mathbb{II}(y)\to 0\hskip 8.50012pt\text{ as }y\to 0.

While for
\mathbb{I}(y)
we consider:

0\leq g_{y}(\tau):=\frac{1}{\tau^{1+s-[s]}}\left\lvert e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{4\tau}}-1\right\rvert\left\lVert P_{\tau}^{H}H^{[s]}f-H^{[s]}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})}.

Clearly, we have
g_{y}(\tau)\to 0
as
y\to 0^{+}
for every
\tau>0
.
On the other hand, it exists a constant
C>0
and a function
g\in L^{1}(0,\infty)
such that
0\leq g_{y}(\tau)\leq Cg(\tau)
for every
\tau>0
. Recalling that
1+s-[s]\in(1,2)
, it suffices to take

g(\tau)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\tau^{s-[s]}}\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 8.50012pt\text{ per }0<\tau\leq 1\\
\frac{1}{\tau^{s-[s]+1}}\hskip 17.00024pt\,\text{ per }1<\tau<\infty,\end{cases}

and from Lebesgue dominated convergence we conclude that
\mathbb{I}(y)\to 0
for
y\to 0^{+}
.

Chapter 5
Conclusion

Considering the proof of the Theorem
4.2.2
, we would like to generalize our result to a broader family of hypoelliptic operators, namely the one introduced by Hörmander
14

\mathscr{K}u:=\text{tr}\,(Q\nabla^{2}u)+\braket{BX,\nabla u}-\partial_{t}u.

(5.1)

It was proven by Hörmander that
\mathscr{K}
is hypoelliptic if and only if the covariance matrix

K(t)=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}e^{sB}Qe^{sB^{*}}\,ds

(5.2)

is invertible, i.e.,
\det K(t)>0
for every
t>0
. In (
5.1
)
Q
and
B
are
N\times N
matrices with real, constant coefficients, with
Q\geq 0,Q=Q^{*}
. We have denoted by
X
the variable in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and by
A^{*}
the transpose of a matrix
A
.

The class of operators (
5.1
) includes several examples of interest in analysis, physics and the applied sciences. The simplest one is of course the ubiquitous heat equation, corresponding to the nondegenerate case when
Q=\mathbb{I}_{N}
,
B=\mathbb{O}_{N}
. When
Q=\mathbb{I}_{N}
,
B=-\mathbb{I}_{N}
one has the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator, which is of great interest in the probability literature. Another example is the degenerate Kolmogorov operator in
\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
with
N=2n
, with the choices
Q=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{I}_{n}&\mathbb{O}_{n}\\
\mathbb{O}_{n}&\mathbb{O}_{n}\end{bmatrix}
, and
B=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{O}_{n}&\mathbb{O}_{n}\\
\mathbb{I}_{n}&\mathbb{O}_{n}\end{bmatrix}
, which arose in the seminal paper
9
on Brownian motion and the theory of gases.

Then, we would like to establish results analogous (at least on the formal level) to Chapter
4
for the nonlocal operators
(-\mathscr{K})^{s}
for
s>0
. In particular, the case
0<s<1
has already been proven by Garofalo and Tralli, as can be seen in
12
.
